L54: ## 1 Executive Summary
L55: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L56: Selumetinib (AZD6244) is an orally bioavailable, non-ATP competitive kinase inhibitor that targets mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK)/extracellular signal regulated kinases 1 and 2 (MEK1/2); the established pharmacological class is kinase inhibitor. MEK1/2 are ubiquitously expressed proteins that are part of the Ras-Raf-MAPK-ERK signaling transduction cascade. Activation of the Ras-MAPK signaling pathway leads to cell proliferation and is involved in a large number of various types of cancer.
L57: Neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) is a syndrome caused by germline mutations in the *NF1* gene resulting in widespread phenotypic outcomes, including plexiform neurofibromas (PN). *NF1* codes for the protein neurofibromin, a widely expressed Ras GTPase activating protein (GAP), that limits downstream Ras signaling. Mutations in *NF1* commonly lead to increased Ras pathway signaling and dysregulated growth. Blocking downstream Ras signaling by targeting MEK1/2 may help slow or reduce plexiform neurofibroma growth.
L58: PNs are nerve sheath tumors that typically grow along large nerves and plexuses and are present at birth. PNs may develop along nerves anywhere in the body, and may be located around the orbit, face, upper and lower limbs, back, thorax, abdomen, neck brachial plexus and/or lumbosacral plexus, which result in clinical symptoms such as disfigurement, motor dysfunction (weakness and restricted range of motion [ROM]), pain, respiratory impairment, visual dysfunction, and neurological dysfunction.
L59: Selumetinib is a new molecular entity that is not approved currently by the United States Food and Drug Administration (The FDA). The drug was granted Orphan Product Designation for the treatment of NF1 in February 2018, and Breakthrough Therapy Designation was granted in March 2019.
L60: The Applicant has proposed the following indication for selumetinib:
L61: *For the treatment of pediatric patients, aged 3 years and above, with neurofibromatosis-1 (NF1) and symptomatic, inoperable plexiform neurofibroma (PN).*
L62: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L63: The current New Drug Application (NDA) for selumetinib was submitted pursuant to Section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act and 21 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR)
L64: 314.126(a)(b). Review teams from all disciplines have recommended that regular approval be granted for selumetinib for the following indication:
L65: *For the treatment of pediatric patients 2 years of age and older with neurofibromatosis-1 (NF1) who have symptomatic, inoperable plexiform neurofibroma (PN).*
L66: The benefit: risk assessment for selumetinib favors the benefit of the drug for the recommended indicated population. The drug has demonstrated a statistically significant, robust, and clinically meaningful effect on ORR that is durable and associated with improvements in disease-related morbidities. These factors represent direct clinical benefit to patients and warrant regular approval of the drug. Additionally, the safety profile for the drug is consistent with other MEK inhibitors and appears acceptable to patients taking the drug who have a serious and life-threatening disease.
L67: The recommended dosing regimen of selumetinib is 25 mg/m2 orally twice daily without food two hours before and one hour after each dose administered continuously until disease progression or intolerable toxicity; for a minimum BSA of 0.55 m2 the recommended dose is 20 mg in the morning and 10 mg in the evening and the recommended dose is capped at 50 mg when the BSA is \ge 1.9 m2. A postmarketing requirement is included as part of the action on this application. Required is the submission of data from a pharmacokinetic trial in pediatric patients to confirm the effect of a low-fat meal on selumetinib exposure with the marketed capsule formulation and evaluate whether administration of selumetinib with food may alleviate gastrointestinal toxicities, and in order to confirm appropriate dosing with a low-fat meal that maintains efficacy with acceptable safety.
L68: The recommendation to approve selumetinib is based on results from a single trial, entitled, “A Phase I/II Study of the Mitogen Activated Protein Kinase (MEK) 1 Inhibitor Selumetinib (AZD6244; HYD Sulfate) in Children with Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF1) and Inoperable Plexiform Neurofibromas (PN)” (SPRINT), specifically from Phase II, Stratum 1 of the trial. SPRINT was an open-label, single arm, multi-center trial conducted by the National Cancer Institute (NCI) Pediatric Oncology Branch (POB). Fifty pediatric patients with NF1 and PN-related morbidity were enrolled. The major efficacy outcome measure for the trial was overall response rate (ORR), or the percentage of patients with complete response (defined as disappearance of the target PN) or confirmed partial response (defined as \ge 20\% reduction in PN volume confirmed at a subsequent tumor assessment within 3-6 months). The results from SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 demonstrated an overall response rate (ORR) per Response Evaluation in Neurofibromatosis and Schwannomatosis (REiNS) criteria of 66% (95% CI: 51, 79) based upon NCI central review, and 44% (95% CI: 30, 59) based upon independent central review (ICR). Among the 33 responding patients included in the NCI assessment, the median duration of response (DOR) was not reached while 82% of patients had a DOR of at least 12 months. Supportive data for the application were derived from the observed ORRs in SPRINT Phase 1 [67% (95% CI: 45, 84)] and SPRINT Phase II Stratum 2 [68% (95% CI: 47, 85)], which were
L69: consistent with the Phase II Stratum 1 results in rate and durability. Additionally, a natural history study of NF1 conducted by the NCI POB was submitted with the application to provide external control data; however, no formal statistical comparisons were made by the FDA. The study did, nevertheless, demonstrate that a key characteristic of NF1 PN is the uncommon occurrence of spontaneous regression such that the observed tumor responses in SPRINT are concluded to be the effect of the drug.
L70: Given the benign, albeit morbid, nature of NF1 PN, clinical outcomes data were also submitted with the application and considered a critical element of the trial to demonstrate the clinical relevance of treatment when associated with the observed radiographic response results. The SPRINT trial evaluated selumetinib effects on PN-related functional impairment, symptoms, and disfigurement using various clinical outcomes assessment (COA) tools and measures as well as photographic evaluations conducted as part of scheduled tumor response assessments. Despite the limitations of the single arm trial design and the relatively small sample sizes available for analysis of specific morbidities, a general trend toward an association between a reduction in tumor volume and durable improvement in clinical outcomes was observed.
L71: The primary safety risks of selumetinib are consistent with the profiles of other approved MEK inhibitors. Adverse Drug Reactions (ADRs) in pediatric patients receiving selumetinib were frequent and varied. Serious adverse events occurred in 24% of patients; those occurring in two or more patients were anemia, hypoxia and diarrhea. Permanent discontinuation resulting from adverse events occurred in 12% of patients, and included increased creatinine, weight increase, diarrhea, paronychia, malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumor (MPNST), acute kidney injury, and skin ulcer. Dose interruptions as a result of adverse reactions occurred in 80% of patients while dose reductions after adverse events occurred in 24% of patients, and those occurring in more than 5% of patients included vomiting, paronychia, diarrhea, nausea, abdominal pain, rash, skin infection, influenza-like illness, pyrexia and weight gain. The most common adverse reactions observed in \ge 40\% of patients were: vomiting, abdominal pain, rash, blood creatine phosphokinase increased, diarrhea, nausea, dry skin, asthenic events, fatigue, musculoskeletal pain, pyrexia, acneiform rash, hypoalbuminemia, and stomatitis.
L72: A risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) is not recommended for selumetinib given the current safety profile and the experience of the medical community in managing expected MEK inhibitor toxicities. Given the intended chronic use of the drug for pediatric patients with NF-1 PN, postmarketing requirements and enhanced pharmacovigilance are included as part of the approval for this NDA. Long-term monitoring of patients will include evaluations for safety, growth and development. Risk management based on labeling as well as routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance will be employed to ensure the safe and effective use of selumetinib in the indicated population.
L73: For the specifics of each discipline's decision on this application, please see the relevant sections of this review, or the separate review filed for that discipline.
L74: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L75: ## **Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment**
L76: Fifty pediatric patients with NF1 and PN-related morbidity were enrolled in Stratum 1 of SPRINT. The results from SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 demonstrated an overall response rate (ORR) per REiNS criteria of 66% (95% CI: 51, 79) based upon NCI central review. and 44% (95% CI: 30, 59) based upon independent central review (ICR). Among the 33 responding patients included in the NCI assessment, the median duration of response (DOR) was not reached while 82% of patients had a DOR of at least 12 months. Supportive data for these findings came from the observed ORRs in SPRINT Phase 1 [67% (95% CI: 45, 84)] and SPRINT Phase II Stratum 2 [68% (95% CI: 47, 85)], which were consistent with the Phase II Stratum 1 results and similarly durable. Additionally, a natural history study of NF1 conducted by the NCI POB and submitted as with the application demonstrated that a key characteristic of NF1 PN is the uncommon occurrence of spontaneous regression such that the observed tumor responses in SPRINT is concluded to be the effect of the drug. The natural history study was submitted to provide external control data; however, no formal statistical comparisons were made.
L77: Clinical outcomes data was also considered a critical element of the trial to demonstrate the clinical relevance of treatment. The SPRINT trial evaluated selumetinib effects on PN-related functional impairment, symptoms, and disfigurement using various COA tools and measures as well as photographic evaluations conducted as part of scheduled tumor response assessments. A general trend toward an association between reduction in tumor volume and durable improvement in clinical outcomes was observed.
L78: The safety population evaluated for selumetinib consisted of a pooled population of 74 pediatric patients who received a dose ranging from 20 mg/m2 to 30 mg/m2 orally twice daily in SPRINT. Among these patients, 74% were exposed for greater than 2 years, and 23% were exposed for greater than 4 years. The safety population also included additional data from 347 adult patients who received selumetinib administered at various doses across a range of tumors in other clinical trials.
L79: For the patient population with NF1 with inoperable PN, the safety assessment population also consisted of the fifty pediatric patients enrolled in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1. Eligible patients were 2-18 years of age with NF1 and inoperable PN that was causing significant morbidity. Patients were excluded for abnormal LVEF, uncontrolled hypertension (blood pressure \ge the 95th percentile for age, height, and sex), any current or past history of RVO or CSR, intraocular pressure &gt; 21 mmHg (or upper limit of normal adjusted by age), uncontrolled glaucoma, and
L80: inability to swallow whole capsules. Patients received selumetinib 25 mg/m2 orally twice daily (n=50). Among these patients, 66% were exposed to the drug for greater than 2 years.
L81: Serious adverse events occurred in 24% of patients; those occurring in two or more patients were anemia, hypoxia and diarrhea. Permanent discontinuation resulting from adverse events occurred in 12% of patients, and included increased creatinine, weight increase, diarrhea, paronychia, malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumor (MPNST), acute kidney injury, and skin ulcer. Dose interruptions as a result of adverse reactions occurred in 80% of patients while dose reductions after adverse events occurred in 24% of patients, and those occurring in more than 5% of patients included vomiting, paronychia, diarrhea, nausea, abdominal pain, rash, skin infection, influenza-like illness, pyrexia and weight gain. The most common adverse reactions observed in ≥ 40% of patients were: vomiting, abdominal pain, rash, blood creatine phosphokinase increased, diarrhea, nausea, dry skin, asthenic events, fatigue, musculoskeletal pain, pyrexia, acneiform rash, hypoalbuminemia, and stomatitis.
L82: The data observed for the durable overall response rates in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 were statistically significant, robust, and clinically meaningful. These results were associated with the COAs assessed during the trial and there was a positive trend observed between tumor shrinkage and the COAs. The safety profile of the drug is consistent with the known safety profile of MEK inhibitors, and the drug has been appropriately labelled for both class effects of the drug and adverse reactions observed in the pediatric population. The benefit: risk assessment for selumetinib is favorable for the treatment of pediatric patients 2 years of age and older with NF1 and symptomatic, inoperable PN a non-malignant disease that causes severe morbidity, is serious, and life threatening.

Analysis of Condition	NF1 is a progressive multi-system disease characterized by progressive cutaneous, neurological, skeletal, and neoplastic manifestations early in life and the associated morbidities can be severe. Patients with NF1 have an increased risk of developing tumors of the central and peripheral nervous system. PNs are one of the most common benign tumors, which occur in approximately 20% to 50% of NF1 patients
NF1 is a rare, progressive, and highly morbid condition that can lead to fatal outcomes depending upon the location of the tumor. It is a serious and life-threatening disease.
L84: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
PNs are nerve sheath tumors that typically grow along large nerves and plexuses and are present at birth. PNs may develop along nerves anywhere in the body, and may be located around the orbit, face, upper and lower limbs, back, thorax, abdomen, neck brachial plexus and/or lumbosacral plexus, which result in clinical symptoms such as disfigurement, motor dysfunction (weakness and restricted range of motion), pain, respiratory impairment, visual dysfunction, and neurological dysfunction.

Safe and effective treatments for this highly morbid condition are needed.

Substantial evidence of effectiveness was demonstrated for selumetinib in the patient population being indicated.

The natural history study was submitted to provide external control data; however, no formal statistical comparisons were made.

Seventy-four percent of patients were exposed to the drug for greater than 2 years, and 23% were exposed for greater than 4 years. The safety population also included additional data from adult and pediatric patients who received selumetinib administered at various doses across a range of tumors in other clinical trials. In a single arm trial, there are no comparisons to be made; however, the safety profile of the drug is consistent with the known safety profile of MEK inhibitors, and the drug has been appropriately labelled for both class effects of the drug and adverse reactions observed in the pediatric population.

L93: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L94: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L95: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L96: Neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1) is a rare, autosomal dominant disorder caused by germline mutations in the NF1 tumor suppressor gene, which encodes the tumor suppressor protein neurofibromin 1. In the United States (US), NF1 has a prevalence of approximately 1 in 2700 (Gutmann et al 2013a, Monroe et al 2017) and a birth incidence of approximately 1:3000 (Ferner et al 2007). Approximately half of NF1 cases are familial, with penetrance being 100%, and the remainder are the result of de novo (spontaneous) mutations (Evans et al 2010). The diagnostic criteria for NF1 were defined at a National Institute of Health (NIH) consensus development conference in 1987 (National Institutes of Health Consensus Development Conference Statement 1987). In most cases, the diagnosis of NF1 is made on clinical grounds, requiring 2 or more clinical features to be present from the defined list of diagnostic criteria for NF1 presented below.
L97: - Six or more café-au-lait macules (diameters \ge 0.5 cm in pre-pubertal patients or \ge 1.5 cm in post-pubertal patients)
- Two or more neurofibromas or 1 plexiform neurofibroma (PN)
- Freckling in axilla or groin
- Optic glioma
- Two or more Lisch nodules (iris hamartomas)
- A distinctive bony lesion (dysplasia of the sphenoid bone or dysplasia or thinning of long bone cortex)
- First-degree relative with NF1 (diagnosed using the above criteria)
L98: NF1 is a progressive multi-system disease characterized by progressive cutaneous, neurological, skeletal, and neoplastic manifestations early in life and the associated morbidities can be severe. Loss of NF1 function favors the GTP-bound active form of RAS, resulting in activation of the RAF/MEK/ERK pathway, which can be the cause of or sustain the growth of various tumor types, such as benign or malignant tumors. Patients with NF1 have an increased risk of developing tumors of the central and peripheral nervous system. PNs are one of the most common benign tumors, which occur in approximately 20% to 50% of NF1 patients (Korf 1999, Mautner et al 2008).
L99: PNs are nerve sheath tumors that typically grow along large nerves and plexuses and are present at birth (Hannema and Oostenbrink 2017). PNs may develop along nerves anywhere in the body, and may be located around the orbit, face, upper and lower limbs, back, thorax,
L100: abdomen, neck brachial plexus and/or lumbosacral plexus, which result in clinical symptoms such as disfigurement, motor dysfunction (weakness and restricted range of motion [ROM]), pain, respiratory impairment, visual dysfunction, and neurological dysfunction.
L101: PN manifestations vary in type and severity. They may continue to become apparent through late adolescence and early adulthood (Williams et al 2009). PN can have heterogenous, complex shapes and sometimes reach very large size, with some documented as being 20% of body weight (Korf 1999, Mautner et al 2008). Patients may have more than one PN, each resulting in different signs and symptoms. PN grow most rapidly during the first decade of life and whilst growth rate is highly variable between patients, the growth rate of PN in younger children is generally much greater compared with that in older children or adults (Dombi et al 2007, Nguyen et al 2012, Tucker et al 2009). A Natural History study of NF1 conducted by the National Cancer Institute (NCI) Pediatric Oncology Branch (POB) has demonstrated that, although spontaneous PN shrinkage occurred in a small number of PN in older adolescent and adult patients, the shrinkage is very slow, over many years and no patient had spontaneous PN shrinkage of \ge 20\% within a year (Dombi et al 2007, Gross et al 2018). Although PNs are benign tumors there is potential for them to undergo malignant transformation; malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumors (MPNST) associated with NF1 most commonly arise in pre-existing PNs (Gutmann et al 2017).
L102: Once PN-related symptoms or morbidities have developed, spontaneous resolution has been shown to be extremely unlikely (Gross et al 2018). Chronic pain in NF1 can be associated with PN, as well as subcutaneous neurofibroma and bony deformations (scoliosis and pseudoarthrosis), and as such, can occur anywhere in the body or multiple sites at the same time (Bellampalli and Khanna 2019).
L103: Importantly, PN can result in life-threatening complications due to compression of vital structures (e.g., great vessel compression, spinal cord compression, and airway obstruction). A retrospective data analysis of the clinical records of children with NF1 reported an increased mortality rate in children with symptomatic PN compared to those without PN or with unrecognized/asymptomatic PN. In this study, non-malignancy-related causes of death in patients with NF1-PN included hypovolemic shock due to a PN-related hemothorax and respiratory failure due to airway compression (Prada et al 2012).
L104: Hence, symptoms from PN affect children from early childhood through adolescence and their entire lifespan, and these clinical complications not only have a considerable effect on patient daily life and can have potentially life-threatening or debilitating consequences but also imposes a societal burden.
L105: In children and adolescents with NF1, research findings indicate reduced quality of life (QOL) relative to population norms and parent-reported ratings tend to yield lower scores in several
L106: domains than child self-report (Garwood et al 2012, Gutmann et al 2017, Oostenbrink et al 2007, Sanagoo et al 2019). A study by Lai et al (2019) investigated health-related quality of life (HRQOL) in the more specific population of 140 children with NF1-related PN. These children reported worse scores than the population norms in the majority of domains, including those relating to physical function, mental health, and social function. Worse depressive symptoms, fatigue and pain interference were reported by children with more PNs (1 to 5 PNs compared to only 1 PN). Furthermore, Wolters et al (2015) found that worse overall QOL was reported in children and adolescents with NF1-PN who had more severe symptoms compared to those with mild symptoms. In addition, with excision of multiple neurofibromas and reconstructive surgery for PN reported as leading reasons for hospitalization in NF1 patients, increasing costs to the health care system should be recognized as an impact to the society (Wolkenstein et al 2000).
L107: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L108: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment of NF1 and NF1 associated PN and emphasizes that although the disease is heterogeneous with regard to number of tumors per patient, size, growth rate, and resulting clinical manifestations, a key characteristic of all PNs is the rare occurrence of spontaneous regression.
L109: PN-associated morbidity depends on the location of the tumor and compression of nearby structures. For example, orbital PNs can displace the globe and compromise vision; paraspinal tumors can compress the spinal cord and cause paralysis; PNs arising in the mediastinum may compress the trachea or great vessels leading to airway compromise; and tumors arising in the extremities can cause progressive neurologic deficit and pain. PNs can negatively impact overall growth and cause significant disfigurement in patients. Literature suggests that patients with symptomatic PN have increased mortality due to compression of airway and other vital structures. Further, although PN is a benign tumor, there is risk for malignant transformation to MPNST.
L110: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L111: ## The Applicant's Position:
L112: There is no approved treatment for NF1-related PN so treatment is either symptomatic (e.g., use of analgesics for pain amelioration) or involves the use of off-label agents which have demonstrated limited benefit (Jakacki et al 2017, Robertson et al 2012). Recently, off-label use of commercialized mitogen-activated protein kinase kinase (MEK) inhibitors has been observed (Papalia et al 2018, Vaassen et al 2019). In suitable patients, surgical excision of the PN may be performed.
L113: Surgical excision is commonly triggered by neurologic, disfigurement, orthopedic, and airway clinical complications caused by PN (Prada et al 2012). However, for many patients surgery is not a viable option as most PNs are not amenable to complete resection due to encasement of, or close proximity to, vital structures (Korf 1999, Needle et al 1997, Packer et al 2002); PN surgery is also associated with high risk of iatrogenic nerve injury and hemorrhage due to the invasiveness or high vascularity of the PN (Canavese and Krajbich 2011). Even with subtotal or partial PN resection, 18% of patients were reported to have experienced permanent surgical complications such as speech abnormalities, nerve palsies, and pain, with regrowth occurring in 55% of these patients (Prada et al 2012). Regrowth has been reported to commonly occur in patients under age 10 (Needle et al 1997). Thus, management of NF1 is often palliative and focused on alleviating symptoms with the use of pain control medications.
L114: Data suggest that 12% of children with PNs take narcotics (Kim et al 2009) and over 70% of children and adults with NF1 use prescription pain medications (Créange et al 1999). Despite pain management being a crucial aspect of improving NF1 patients' QOL, no approved therapies exist for NF1-induced pain, and in the limited studies that evaluated the efficacy of analgesics or other pain management techniques in NF1, patients reported that available treatments were not beneficial. In total, 93% of NF1 adolescent patients with PN and 100% of their caregivers reported pain interference with daily functioning even after taking over-the-counter pain medications including acetaminophen, ibuprofen, and prescription pain medications including various opioids, anticonvulsants, antidepressants, anesthetics on a regular basis (Wolters et al 2015).
L115: Treatment and clinical management options for NF1 PN are extremely limited. To date several Phase II clinical studies of targeted agents for progressive PN in children and young adults with NF1 have failed to demonstrate consistent or durable PN volume decreases (Gutmann et al 2013a). There are no approved medical treatments in the US or worldwide that have been shown to prevent, cure, slow, or reverse the growth of NF1-related PNs. Therefore, there is a recognized need for a safe and efficacious medicine for patients with NF1, particularly for those with significant morbidity due to growth of PNs.
L116: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L117: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's discussion of current treatment options for patients with NF1 PN and has the following additional comments.
L118: There are currently no FDA-approved systemic therapies for treatment of PN. Off-label use of cytotoxic chemotherapy is generally not recommended due to risk for secondary malignancies and other safety concerns in a population with a benign tumor. Activity-estimating studies of various tyrosine kinase inhibitors, mTOR inhibitors, a farnesyltransferase inhibitor, and anti-inflammatory and immune modulating agents have not demonstrated meaningful or durable objective responses in patients with progressive PN (Jakacki et al 2017, Kim et al 2013, Robertson et al 2012, Weiss et al 2014, Widemann et al 2014).
L119: Given the underlying pathogenesis of NF1, specifically the absence of functional neurofibromin and the resulting upregulation of RAS signaling of the RAF/MEK/ERK pathway, preclinical studies of drugs for NF1-related tumors have focused on agents that interfere with RAS and downstream pathways of RAS. MEK inhibitors have demonstrated antitumor activity in mouse models of NF1 PN, and there are case reports of off-label use of FDA-approved MEK inhibitors leading to objective responses in patients with PN (Jessen 2013, Papalia et al 2018, Vaassen et al 2019). Multiple MEK inhibitors, including selumetinib, trametinib, binimetinib, and PD-0325901 are currently under investigation in ongoing clinical trials in pediatric and adult patients with NF1 PN and other NF1 related tumors (NCT03962543, NCT03231306, NCT02407405, NCT03363217, NCT03741101, NCT02124772).
L120: ## 3 Regulatory Background
L121: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L122: ## The Applicant's Position:
L123: Selumetinib is not currently registered (or approved) in the US or in any other part of the world. The current application was submitted by the Sponsor as a new drug application (NDA) on 13 September 2019.
L124: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L125: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment.

L136: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L137: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's regulatory timeline and meeting types presented in Table 1 and provides the following additional comments regarding key discussions that occurred at specific meetings and other important regulatory submissions and communications prior to submission of NDA 213756.
L138: ## *Type B Pre-IND meeting - September 8, 2014*
L139: A pre-IND meeting was held among the FDA, the NCI, and the Applicant to discuss the development program for selumetinib for pediatric patients with inoperable NF1-related PN based on preliminary clinical results from the ongoing NCI-sponsored Study 11-C-0161 (CTEP#8799; SPRINT): "A phase 1 study of the mitogen activated protein kinase (MEK) 1 inhibitor AZD6244 hydrogen sulfate (selumetinib sulfate) in children with NF1 and inoperable plexiform neurofibromas." This study was conducted under IND (b) (4) NCI, with the support of the Applicant, proposed to amend the ongoing SPRINT study with the incorporation of a single arm expansion cohort to further investigate the safety and effectiveness of selumetinib in pediatric patients with NF1-related PN. The proposed primary endpoint was radiographic response using volumetric MRI analysis according to Response Evaluation in Neurofibromatosis and Schwannomatosis (REiNS) criteria. The Applicant also proposed to measure clinical outcome assessment (COA) endpoints as exploratory endpoints to further characterize the benefit:risk profile and stated in the meeting briefing document that these results would not be included in product labeling claims. The results of the proposed expansion arm were intended to support a marketing application for selumetinib in pediatric patients with inoperable NF1 PN. NCI also provided supportive preliminary data from the ongoing NCI POB NF1 Natural History study. The following key points were discussed:
L140: - The FDA stated that if the prospectively collected data from the ongoing NCI natural history study demonstrate a consistent PN growth rate, evidence that spontaneous tumor regression does not occur, and that improvement or worsening in measurable functional impairments correlate with decreases or increases, respectively, in tumor volumetric changes of \ge 20\%, this information could be sufficient to establish the natural history of the disease such that observed effects during the proposed study could be attributed to the selumetinib treatment effect and not the natural history of disease.
- The FDA stated that for the proposed 20% reduction in tumor volume to be considered clinically relevant, it will be essential to show that this change in volume is reliably
L141: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213756
## Koselugo (selumetinib)
L142: accompanied by a detectable effect on physical function or other measures indicating that the patient's quality of life is improved. The FDA acknowledged that the protocol could include multiple COA tools for supportive efficacy assessments due to various tumor locations (e.g., vision testing for orbital tumors, strength testing for spinal tumors, photography for disfiguring lesions, etc.) such that there would likely be more than one set of COA criteria incorporated into the protocol.
L143: - FDA stated that given the rarity of NF1 PN, lack of treatment alternatives, and the debilitating complications arising from progressive PN, and provided that the natural history of disease has been adequately established through the NCI natural history study, a single-arm trial could support an application seeking marketing approval if the study demonstrates unequivocal evidence of an important treatment effect that results in an improvement in PN-related functional impairment or other well-characterized signs or symptoms of progressive PN disease. The FDA emphasized that the results of the trial must also demonstrate that treatment responses are sufficiently durable, considering the long-term survival of patients with NF1 PN.
L144: On September 4, 2015, the SPRINT study under NCI-sponsored IND (b) (4) was amended to include the phase 2 expansion cohort discussed at the preIND meeting. The amended study included two groups within the expansion phase: Stratum 1 was for the enrollment of 50 patients with a measurable PN-related morbidity, and Stratum 2 was for the enrollment of 25 patients without significant PN-related morbidity at enrollment but whose tumors impose risk for development of morbidity. The primary endpoint was ORR according to MRI volumetric analysis using REINS criteria.
L145: ## *Type C Meeting – November 7, 2017*
L146: A Type C meeting between the FDA and the Applicant was held to discuss the Applicant's planned analyses for ORR, duration of response (DOR), safety, and COA endpoints from the SPRINT trial in preparation for submission of a marketing application. The following key points were made:
L147: - The FDA agreed that the planned descriptive analyses of the changes in the various COAs could be used as supportive data in characterizing the clinical benefit of selumetinib. The FDA stated that the proposed COAs were considered exploratory in the single-arm trial design and were unlikely to support labeling claims.
- The FDA recommended an additional response analysis be conducted after all patients had prolonged follow-up (i.e., at least 12 months from the onset of response) to better characterize the ORR and durability of response.
L148: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213756
## Koselugo (selumetinib)
L149: - FDA stated that the general expectation for imaging endpoints used in a single-arm trial intended to support approval is blinded, independent confirmation of the ORR and DOR results.
L150: ## *Type C Meeting – July 18, 2018*
L151: A Type C meeting between the FDA and the Applicant was held to discuss potential trial conduct and data quality issues based on the Applicant's conduct of quality assurance audits of the SPRINT trial sites and the proposed risk mitigation plans to ensure reliability of the data. One concern was that a subset of patients had a portion of protocol-required safety assessments at local centers rather than at the SPRINT trial sites for patient convenience. The Applicant agreed to conduct and submit subgroup safety analyses for patients who received study-related evaluations at one of the trial clinical sites and for patients who had non-re-staging visits at local centers. The FDA generally agreed with the other proposed plans to address potential trial conduct issues and stated that whether the mitigation procedures can allow for full confidence in the reliability of the results to support marketing approval will be determined during review of the application.
L152: ## *Type C Meeting – November 13, 2018 (Written Response)*
L153: On August 29, 2018, the Applicant submitted a pre-NDA meeting request. The final topline results for DOR from SPRINT were not available. As such, the FDA could not agree to the final content of the planned NDA submission and therefore granted a Type C guidance meeting. Written responses to the Applicant's questions regarding the structure, format and content of the future selumetinib NDA submission were issued on November 13, 2018. The following key agreements and recommendations were made:
L154: - The FDA agreed with the Applicant's plan to analyze ORR according to the REiNS volumetric MRI response criteria based on a blinded, independent review of images, the Applicant's analysis of the NCI central review of tumor volume data to derive ORR and DOR, and the original central NCI assessment.
- The FDA noted that the results of the independent review of ORR and DOR would likely be used as the primary regulatory analysis in the context of a single-arm trial.
- The FDA agreed with the Applicant's proposal to not pool the efficacy data from the phase 1 and 2 parts of the SPRINT study due to differences in design and patient characteristics; however, recommended that the Applicant conduct supportive efficacy analyses using SPRINT phase 1 results, and that these analyses be submitted and presented in the Summary of Clinical Efficacy (SCE).
- The FDA stated that the Applicant's plan to reference tumor volume data from the NCI NF1 Natural History study and the placebo arm from Part A of the NCI Study 01-C-0222 as
L155: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213756
## Koselugo (selumetinib)
L156: external controls for tumor growth and for the efficacy endpoints progression free survival (PFS) and time to progression (TTP), respectively was acceptable but noted that these analyses would be considered exploratory.
L157: - The FDA stated that the Applicant's proposal for the analyses of COAs based on the descriptive statistics and mixed model repeated measures (MMRM) appeared acceptable but noted that the open-label trial design limits interpretability of COA data because of patient's (or parent/caregiver's) knowledge of treatment assignment, lack of a comparator, small sample size, and potential instrument limitations. The FDA acknowledged the challenges of measuring clinical outcomes in diseases that are rare and heterogeneous across patients and stated that submitted COA data would be reviewed as part of the totality of data.
- The FDA agreed with the proposal to submit individual patient reviews (IPRs) as supportive efficacy data. The reviews were to include relevant COA results in the context of radiographic response data for each patient. The FDA advised that in each IPR, the Applicant include a discussion of how the change in the target function or symptom correlated with the radiographic response assessment. The FDA also requested that each IPR include a discussion of the patient's safety and tolerability of selumetinib to allow for an individual benefit: risk assessment.
L158: ## *Breakthrough Therapy Designation – March 29, 2019*
L159: On January 30, 2019, the Applicant submitted a Breakthrough Therapy Designation (BTD) Request for selumetinib for the treatment of pediatric patients aged 3 years and above, with NF1 symptomatic and/or progressive, inoperable PN. The primary data supporting the BTD request for selumetinib came from 50 patients enrolled in SPRINT Phase 2, stratum 1. The FDA considered the data provided in the BTD request to be preliminary clinical evidence of a substantial improvement over the local control surgical options and supportive care therapies available for inoperable and morbid PN. The effect sizes on ORR and DOR were noted to be substantially larger than those observed in clinical studies of other products evaluated in similar study populations using the same volumetric response criteria. Additionally, the Applicant submitted preliminary results for pain and motor dysfunction evaluations from a subset of patients in SPRINT that suggested trends of improved strength and motor function and decreases in pain that correlated with reductions in tumor volume during selumetinib exposure. The FDA additionally considered published results from the NCI Natural History study suggesting that increases in tumor volume correlate with increased patient morbidity and that symptomatic patients generally do not have improvement in tumor related morbidity when there is no change in tumor volume. On March 29, 2019, the FDA granted BTD to selumetinib for the treatment of pediatric patients aged 3 years and above, with NF1 and inoperable PN that is causing significant morbidity.
L160: ## *Type B Pre-NDA meeting - June 28, 2019*
L161: A Type B Pre-NDA meeting was held among the FDA, the NCI, and the Applicant to come to agreement on the format and content of the complete application. The following key points were discussed:
L162: - The FDA agreed that the efficacy data from SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, including ORR, DOR, and COA results, could support filing of a marketing application for selumetinib for the treatment of pediatric patients with NF1 and inoperable PN that is causing significant morbidity. The FDA stated that the final indication would depend on review of the safety and efficacy data for all patients and relevant subgroups.
- The FDA agreed with the Applicant's plan to conduct and submit a supportive efficacy analysis comparing the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 response results to the proposed external control data from the NCI POB NF1 Natural History Study.
- The FDA did not agree to the Applicant's proposal to exclude SPRINT Phase II Stratum 2 efficacy and safety data from the NDA submission. During the meeting discussion, the FDA acknowledged that efficacy data from this cohort may be difficult to compare to the efficacy data from Stratum 1 due to shorter follow-up but asked the Applicant to provide preliminary ORR and DOR data from Stratum 2 in the 90-day safety update. The Applicant agreed to submit these data with the 90-day safety update. The FDA agreed with the Applicant's proposal to provide SAE line listings as the only safety data from Stratum 2 I the NDA with an update to these listings as part of the safety update.
- The FDA did not object to the Applicant's proposal to retain the NCI central analysis of ORR and DOR as the primary endpoint; however, reiterated that the FDA generally considers the results of independent review of ORR and DOR to be most free of bias and therefore most relevant for inclusion in product labeling. The FDA recommended that the Applicant provide, in the NDA, a rationale for their selection of the NCI central analysis of ORR and DOR as the primary efficacy analysis.
- The FDA acknowledged the multifactorial nature of detected changes in COAs during the study, but emphasized that in order to demonstrate a favorable benefit:risk assessment for selumetinib in a benign condition in which tumor shrinkage will not be predictive of improved survival, there should be a general correlation between decrease in tumor volume and improvement in disease-related functional impairment, symptoms, or an improvement in disfiguring tumors, to make the ORR results clinically relevant.
- The FDA stated that the acute and long-term safety of selumetinib in the intended patient population will also be considered in the overall benefit:risk assessment.

L176: ## 5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L177: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L178: Selumetinib (AZD6244) is an orally bioavailable, non-ATP competitive kinase inhibitor that targets mitogen-activated protein kinase (MAPK)/extracellular signal regulated kinases 1 and 2 (MEK1/2); the established pharmacological class is kinase inhibitor. MEK1/2 are ubiquitously expressed proteins that are part of the Ras-Raf-MAPK-ERK signaling transduction cascade. Activation of the Ras-MAPK signaling pathway leads to proliferation in a large number of cancer types. Neurofibromatosis type I (NF1) is a syndrome caused by germline mutations in the *NF1* gene that result in widespread phenotypic outcomes, including plexiform neurofibromas. *NF1* codes for the protein neurofibromin, a widely expressed Ras GTPase activating protein (GAP). Ras GAPs catalyze the conversion of Ras from the GTP-bound active conformation to the GDP inactive conformation, thereby limiting downstream Ras signaling. Mutations in *NF1* commonly lead to increased Ras pathway signaling and dysregulated growth. Blocking downstream Ras signaling by targeting MEK1/2 may help circumvent the effects of losing neurofibromin GAP activity Kiuru, M and Busam, KJ (2017), including slowing or reducing plexiform neurofibroma growth.
L179: In in vitro assays, selumetinib was able to inhibit the activity of purified MEK1 enzyme with IC_{50} values of between 10-14 nM but was inactive, or only minimally active, against a panel of other kinases at a concentration of 10 \muM. Selumetinib had similar activity against both MEK1 and MEK2 in inhibiting the phosphorylation of inactive ERK2. The Applicant also demonstrated that the N-desmethyl selumetinib metabolite was able to inhibit MEK-mediated ERK phosphorylation at lower concentrations than selumetinib itself; the selumetinib amide metabolite also inhibited ERK phosphorylation but not at clinically relevant concentrations. In vivo, selumetinib reduced proximal nerve root sizes as well as the number and size of tumors in a *Nf1fl/fl;DhhCre*, a mouse model which allows for targeted knockout of *NF1* and recapitulates the development of endogenous neurofibromas. These studies support the proposed mechanism of action of selumetinib and the proof-of-concept for the proposed pediatric indication.
L180: The Applicant conducted several stand-alone safety pharmacology studies with selumetinib. At doses of up to 100 mg/kg selumetinib had no remarkable effects on open field behavior or respiratory parameters in Sprague-Dawley rats. In a cardiovascular safety study in Gottingen minipigs, oral administration of selumetinib twice daily had no remarkable effects on electrocardiograms or circulatory function (heart rate, systolic and diastolic blood pressure) following limited dosing. These findings were consistent with the low potential for QT prolongation predicted by an in vitro hERG assay (IC_{50}&gt;10 \muM) as well as the lack of cardiac findings in the long-term monkey toxicology study. Minimal to mild mineralization associated
L181: with elevated serum phosphorous was frequently present at multiple dose levels, particularly in rats and monkeys. In rats, this included moderate mineralization of the aorta at dose levels resulting in exposures \ge 93 times the clinical exposure at the 25 mg/m^2 twice daily dose.
L182: The Applicant evaluated the toxicity profile of selumetinib in rats, mice, and monkeys in GLP-compliant studies with durations of up to 3 months (rats) and 26 weeks (mice and monkeys). The Applicant chose the mouse as the rodent species for the long-term toxicology and developmental studies due to detectable levels of the active N-desmethyl metabolite in this species that allowed for toxicological coverage; there were no unique human metabolites or other metabolites that needed toxicological coverage in animals. The gastrointestinal (GI) tract was a major target organ in all 3 species. In stand-alone single-dose safety pharmacology studies in rats selumetinib induced dose-dependent gastric mucosal lesions and showed a mild increase in gastric motility. GI toxicity in the 26-week chronic mouse toxicology study ranged from mild inflammation and hyperplasia at the low dose of 1 mg/kg twice daily (approximately 2-3 times the human exposure based on AUC at the clinical dose of 25 mg/m^2 twice daily), to ulcerative lesions at the 20 mg/kg highest dose level. In the 3-month rat study, GI findings were primarily related to mineralization, while in monkeys there were clinical observations of watery-liquid stool and dehydration at selumetinib exposures greater than or equal to the clinical AUC at the 25 mg/m^2 twice daily dose. Gastrointestinal toxicity with diarrhea is a common clinical finding.
L183: In addition to the GI tract, the other major target organs in the rodent toxicology studies were the skin and lower urinary tract. In the long-term 26-week toxicology study, mice received up to 20 mg/kg of selumetinib orally twice daily. Male mice did not tolerate the 20 mg/kg dose (approximately 33 times the human exposure at steady state of 1958 ng*h/mL at the clinical dose of 25 mg/m^2 twice daily), primarily due to urinary flow obstruction leading to death or early sacrifice of all males at the dose level by Week 16. In rats, the highest levels of tissue mineralization were in the kidneys. Clinical observations of skin lesions and scabs correlated with histopathologic findings of skin ulcerations and contributed to the termination of the rat study at selumetinib dose levels \ge 20 mg/kg. Elevated neutrophils and monocytes in rats were most likely secondary to inflammatory responses due to skin lesions/scabs and ulcerations. Skin toxicity is also a common clinical finding.
L184: While the plexiform neurofibromas associated with NF1 can be debilitating, they are generally slow growing and not immediately life-threatening (depending on location). Because of these characteristics, the Applicant conducted a full battery of reproductive toxicology studies as well as carcinogenicity studies to support the development of selumetinib. The Applicant assessed the effects of selumetinib on reproduction and development using mice, as mice, unlike rats, make the active N-desmethyl AZD6244 metabolite at detectable levels. Administration of selumetinib for 10 weeks had no effect on male fertility based on sperm counts and motility as well as fertility indices in untreated females paired with the treated males. At exposures higher
L185: than clinical exposures, selumetinib did not have any clear effects on fertility indices in female mice treated for 2 weeks prior to mating through 6 days post-coitum. Selumetinib did, however, have developmental effects in both embryo-fetal development and pre- and postnatal development studies. In the embryo-fetal development study, doses greater than the lowest tested dose of 2.5 mg/kg twice daily (~5 times the human exposure based on AUC at the clinical dose of 25 mg/m2 twice daily) selumetinib caused increased post-implantation loss, decreased fetal weights, and unossified or incompletely ossified skull bones as well as an increased incidence of cleft palate and open eyes. The Applicant attributed the incidence of open eyes at the 2.5 mg/kg dose level of selumetinib to a dosing error, resulting in the delivery of a higher dose at a critical timepoint for eyelid development. While FDA does not dispute this attribution, there were also findings of prematurely open eyes in the postnatal development study even at the lowest maternal dose level of 0.5 mg/kg twice daily (dam plasma concentrations 2 hours post-dose on lactation Day 4, of ~0.6 times the human Cmax at the clinical dose of 25 mg/m2 twice daily), suggesting that there is some potential for ocular effects at even lower doses of selumetinib. Findings in fetuses from dams at the high dose level of 37.5 mg/kg twice daily also included an increased incidence of folded retina and changes to the consistency of the lens. Similarly, in the pre- and postnatal development study there were dose-dependent increases in the number of litters with fetuses that had prematurely open eyes at doses \ge 0.5 mg/kg as well as a decrease in the number of pups with pupil constriction on post-natal Day 21. While ocular effects were not clear in the nonclinical toxicology studies in adult animals, ocular toxicity does occur clinically with selumetinib and other MEK inhibitors. In addition, there are literature reports supporting a role for MAPK signaling, particularly after activation through FGFR receptors, in protecting/maintaining retinal pigment epithelial cells (Guillonneau, X. et al. 1998; Rosenthal, R. et al. 2005; and Van der Noll, R. et al. 2013). Finally, at maternal selumetinib doses \ge7.5 mg/kg (approximately equal to the clinical exposure at 25 mg/m2 twice daily) there were decreased fetal weights at birth; body weights in these pups remained lower during the post-weaning period. The Applicant also demonstrated that selumetinib was present in milk in this study: selumetinib was detectable in untreated pups from treated dams 2 hours post-dosing on postpartum Day 4, at plasma concentrations of ~0.09 times the Cmax at the clinical dose.
L186: In genetic toxicology studies, selumetinib was positive in the in vivo mammalian erythrocyte micronucleus test at dose levels &gt;160 mg/kg for the free-base formulation or 24 mg/kg for the hyd-sulfate formulation (both doses resulting in maximal concentrations or estimated maximal concentrations &gt;38 times the Cmax at the 160 mg twice daily clinical dose), but negative in both the presence and absence of metabolic activation for mutagenicity in the in vitro Ames Assay and for chromosomal aberrations in the in vitro mammalian cell gene mutation assay using the L5178Y/TK+/- mouse lymphoma cells. Selumetinib had no carcinogenic potential in dedicated studies in mice and rats.
L187: Because the positive genotoxicity findings occurred only at doses approximately 40 times
L188: higher (based on BSA) than the clinical dose and considering that the drug was negative in carcinogenicity studies, FDA made recommendations for use of effective contraception and avoidance of breastfeeding during treatment with selumetinib and for 1 week following the final dose, consistent with the recommendations described in the FDA guidance, Oncology Pharmaceuticals: Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations, for a non-genotoxic drug. There are no outstanding issues from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective that would prevent the approval of selumetinib for the treatment of patients with NF1 who have symptomatic, inoperable plexiform neurofibromas.

L194: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L195: Selumetinib (formerly known as AZD6244, AZ12252244, and ARRY-142886) has been evaluated in a comprehensive package of in vitro and in vivo nonclinical studies that were designed to characterize the pharmacology, safety pharmacology, pharmacokinetics (PK) and toxicology of this compound. Two forms of selumetinib have been used in the nonclinical development studies. Pharmacology (primary and secondary), and safety pharmacology studies were conducted using selumetinib free base. Due to the limited solubility of the selumetinib free base and the resulting low bioavailability, selumetinib hydrogen sulfate salt (hyd-sulfate) was developed and used in later studies. Selumetinib has an active metabolite, N-desmethyl selumetinib, which has also been characterized throughout nonclinical development.
L196: ## Primary Pharmacology
L197: The Applicant evaluated the enzymatic activity of purified constitutively active MEK1, in the presence of increasing concentrations of selumetinib (AZD6244), by measuring the incorporation of [\gamma-33P]phosphate from [\gamma-33P]ATP into ERK2 (Pharmacology Report #01; Yeh, TC. et al (2007); Clin Cancer Res 13:1576-1583). AZD6244 demonstrated concentration-dependent inhibition of MEK enzyme activity (Figure 1). The IC50 was 14.1 nM (n = 9). Evaluation of the selectivity of AZD6244 (10 \muM) against a panel of 40 other serine/threonine and tyrosine kinases showed minimal or no inhibition. In the presence of increasing concentrations of ATP, AZD6244 was non-competitive with respect to ATP (data not shown).
L198: **Figure 1 Inhibition of MEK1 Enzyme Activity in the Presence of Increasing Concentrations of Selumetinib**
L199: (Applicant Figure excerpted from Yeh, TC. et al (2007); Clin Cancer Res 13:1576-1583)
L200: In Pharmacology Reports #45 and #46, the Applicant confirmed the ability of AZD6244 to inhibit the ability of both MEK1 and MEK2 to phosphorylate ERK using two assays: the Z'-LYTE® biochemical assay which employs a fluorescence-based, coupled-enzyme format, based on the differential sensitivity of phosphorylated and non-phosphorylated peptides to proteolytic cleavage (Assay 1) and the radiometric assay described above to measure the phosphorylation of ERK1/2 (Assay 2). AZD6244 prevented phosphorylation of ERK by both MEK1 and MEK2 with similar potency (Table 2).

L206: Investigators further explored the ability of AZD6244 to inhibit MEK1/2 by measuring the phosphorylation state of ERK1/2 (phospho-ERK), the direct substrates of MEK1/2, in treated human cell lines (Pharmacology Report #02). AZD6244 inhibited ERK1/2 phosphorylation in 9 of 10 cell lines examined, with IC50 values \le10 nM (Table 3).

L210: Using the same methods, investigators assessed the ability of the N-desmethyl AZD6244 metabolite and the amide metabolite, to inhibit ERK proliferation in cell lines (Pharmacology Reports #05 and #08). N-desmethyl AZD6244 was more potent than AZD6244 in inhibiting phosphorylation of ERK in seven tumor cell lines (Table 4). In contrast, the AZD6244 amide was more than 30-fold less potent than AZD6244 (Table 5), suggesting that though this metabolite is active, it is unlikely to contribute significantly to the activity of AZD6244.

L215: To further explore the ability of both AZD6244 and its N-desmethyl metabolite to inhibit MEK, investigators conducted an ex-vivo experiment using phorbol ester (TPA) to stimulate ERK phosphorylation in whole blood cells (Pharmacology Report #7). The N-desmethyl AZD6244 metabolite inhibited ERK phosphorylation at a slightly lower IC50 than AZD6244 (Table 6), confirming the activity of both AZD6244 and this metabolite.

L218: The Applicant evaluated the in vivo antitumor effects of AZD6244 using the *Nf1*flox/flox; *Postn-Cre* mouse model of neurofibroma (Pharmacology Report #55). These mice are genetically engineered mice to lose *Nf1* gene expression in Schwann cell progenitors and develop neurofibromas with a histology that replicates human neurofibroma histology by 4 months of age. Four-month old *Nf1*flox/flox; *Postn-Cre* mice received AZD6244 (10 mg/kg) or vehicle orally twice daily to for 12 weeks. AZD6244 reduced both the size of the proximal nerve roots (37% decreases compared to vehicle controls) and the tumor numbers (75 % compared to controls) (Figure 2).

L224: ## Secondary Pharmacology
L225: Selumetinib and N-desmethyl selumetinib were tested at 10 \muM in a panel of in vitro radioligand binding and enzyme assays covering a diverse range of molecular targets, including enzymes, receptors, transporters and ion channels. Selumetinib had significant activity (defined as &gt;50% inhibition) in 1 of the 305 assays in which it was tested (protein serine/threonine kinase PRKG1, 53% inhibition at 10 \muM). N-desmethyl selumetinib had significant activity in 1 of the 336 assays in which it was tested (protein serine/threonine kinase MEK1, 84% inhibition at 10 \muM).
L226: ## The Applicant's Position:
L227: In a secondary pharmacology panel of radioligand binding and enzyme assays, no targets were identified that were considered pharmacologically relevant for human therapeutic plasma exposure levels of selumetinib or N-desmethyl selumetinib.
L228: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L229: FDA confirmed the Applicant's findings. The screening assays covered a sufficient range of targets and did not demonstrate significant selumetinib or N-desmethyl selumetinib-mediated activity against proteins other than MEK1/2.
L230: ## Safety Pharmacology
L231: ## **Central Nervous System**
L232: The effects of selumetinib on general behavioral, autonomic, neuromuscular and sensorimotor function and body temperature were assessed in an Irwin screen in conscious male rats (1758/ARR/03) for up to 24 hours after a single oral dose of 0 (vehicle), 10, 30 or 100 mg/kg selumetinib. There were no effects on body temperature or Irwin observations that were considered selumetinib-related. Therefore, the no-observed-effect-level (NOEL) was 100 mg/kg.
L233: ## **Cardiovascular System**
L234: In vitro evaluation (030819.BCP and 0179SZ): Selumetinib was tested for its effect on the human ether-à-go-go-related gene (hERG) encoded potassium channel over a nominal concentration range of 0.1 to 10 µM. Selumetinib did not cause notable inhibition of the hERG current (mean\pmSEM: 8.2%\pm0.8% inhibition at 10 µM).
L235: N-desmethyl selumetinib was tested for its effect on the hERG encoded potassium channel over a nominal concentration range of 0.03 to 100 µM. N-desmethyl selumetinib did not inhibit the hERG current over this concentration range.
L236: In vivo evaluation (Study 1759/ARR/03): The effects of selumetinib on arterial blood pressure (BP), heart rate and lead II electrocardiogram (ECG) (QT, heart rate corrected QT interval [QTc] and PR intervals) were determined. Cardiovascular parameters were monitored by telemetry from 4 male conscious Göttingen minipigs prior to, and up to 24 hours following the first administration of the day for each dose level. Each dose level of selumetinib was dosed twice daily (BID), with a minimum of 12 hours between the 2 administrations. Oral doses of 0 (vehicle control), 3, 10 and 30 mg/kg BID selumetinib were administered according to a cross-over design, with an interval of at least 2 days between dose levels.
L237: Selumetinib total (and free, based on minipig protein binding of 93.7%) group mean plasma maximum observed plasma concentration (C_{max}) values were 92 (5.80), 126 (7.94) and 408 (25.7) ng/mL following administration of 3, 10 and 30 mg/kg BID selumetinib, respectively. There were no changes in cardiovascular parameters that were considered selumetinib-related. Therefore, the NOEL was 30 mg/kg BID.
L238: ## Respiratory System
L239: The effects of selumetinib on respiratory rate, tidal and minute volume, lung dynamic compliance and airway resistance were assessed in anaesthetized male rats (Study 1760/ARR/03) for up to 2 hours following a single oral gavage administration of 0 (vehicle), 10, 30 or 100 mg/kg selumetinib. Respiratory parameters were recorded in the spontaneously breathing rats via pressure transducers surgically placed in the trachea and plural cavity. Single oral administration of up to 100 mg/kg selumetinib produced no effect on tidal or minute volume, respiratory rate or lung dynamic compliance. At 100 mg/kg, airway resistance was slightly increased (18% compared to vehicle) only at the 2 hours post-dose time point. The NOEL was 30 mg/kg.
L240: ## Gastrointestinal System
L241: Gastrointestinal motility in rats (1761/ARR/03): The effects of selumetinib on gastrointestinal motility were assessed in male rats after a single oral dose of 0 (vehicle), 10, 30 or 100 mg/kg selumetinib. A charcoal suspension was administrated orally 30-minutes post-dose, and the distance the charcoal travelled along the intestinal tract in 20 minutes was then measured. Selumetinib slightly increased the distance of charcoal meal travelled at the 3 doses tested. However, these effects were not statistically significant compared to vehicle and therefore were not considered to be related to selumetinib treatment. The NOEL was therefore considered to be 100 mg/kg.
L242: Gastric irritation in rats (1762/ARR/03): The effects of selumetinib on gastric irritation were assessed in male rats after a single oral dose of 0 (vehicle), 10, 30 or 100 mg/kg selumetinib. Macroscopic evaluation for lesions in the stomach was made approximately 4 hours after dosing. Gastric mucosal lesions were observed at all dose levels. The incidence and severity of gastric findings was similar in animals dosed with vehicle or 10 mg/kg of selumetinib. In animals dosed 30 or 100 mg/kg selumetinib, there was an increase in the incidence and severity of gastric lesions, which at the 100 mg/kg dose, was similar to the positive control (aspirin 20 mg/kg followed by 1 mL/kg 0.4 molar hydrochloric acid). The NOEL was therefore considered to be 10 mg/kg.
L243: Gastric secretion in rats (1763/ARR/03): The effects of selumetinib on gastric secretion were assessed in anaesthetized male rats after a single oral dose of 0 (vehicle), 10, 30 or 100 mg/kg selumetinib. Sixty minutes post-dose, animals were anaesthetized and prepared for assessment, and gastric secretion was subsequently evaluated by measuring the volume, pH and acidity of the gastric juices 4 hours later. Selumetinib had no effect on the gastric secretion parameters. The NOEL was 100 mg/kg.
L244: ## The Applicant's Position:
L245: Selumetinib has been evaluated in a battery of in vivo safety pharmacology studies to evaluate potential effects on the cardiovascular, respiratory, central nervous system (CNS) and
L246: gastrointestinal function. Selumetinib and N-desmethyl selumetinib were also evaluated in a hERG assay and a secondary pharmacology panel of targets in vitro. There were no toxicologically significant findings in these studies and assays.
L247: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L248: FDA confirmed the Applicants' assessment that acute oral administration of selumetinib did not have any clear effects on neurological, cardiovascular, or respiratory parameters in rats or minipigs, but did affect gastrointestinal (GI) endpoints in rats. These GI effects included a mild increase in gastric motility at doses \ge 10 mg/kg (Table 7); though this increase did not reach statistical significance) and a dose-dependent increase in mucosal lesions at doses \ge 30 mg/kg.

L252: ## 5.4. Absorption, Distribution, Metabolism, Excretion/Pharmacokinetics
L253: ## Data:
L254: ## **Absorption**
L255: *7 Day Oral Dose Range Finding Study in the Mouse (Study 0354DM)*
L256: *The pharmacokinetics of AZD6244 and N-desmethyl-AZD6244 in the cynomolgus monkey following oral and intravenous administration (Study KKP023)*
L257: Selumetinib hydrogen sulfate (hyd-sulfate) showed higher exposure and approximately dose proportional increase in exposure compared with the free base which demonstrated dose limited absorption. In mice after 50 mg/kg BID dose of selumetinib hyd-sulfate time to maximum observed plasma concentration (t_{max}) was 3.8 hours and 1 hour respectively in males and females, with similar C_{max} of 55 and 63 \mug/mL and area under the plasma concentration-time curve from time 0 to 12 hours (AUC_{(0-12)}) 271 and 258 \mug.h/mL respectively. In monkey at 5 mg/kg t_{max} was 0.5 hours to 1 hour for the hyd-sulfate and mean C_{max} and area under the plasma concentration-time curve from time 0 to 24 hours (AUC_{(0-24)}) were 2.55 \mug/mL and 3.46 \mug.h/mL, respectively with half-life of circa (approximately; ca.) 5.6 hours. The mean bioavailability was 56% with hyd-sulfate, compared to 29% for the free base.
L258: ## Distribution
L259: The *in vitro* binding of [14C]-AZD6244 to the plasma proteins of mouse, rat, dog, minipig, monkey and man and to specific plasma proteins and the association of [14C]-AZD6244 with the blood cells of rat, dog, monkey and man (Study KPJ003)
L260: The tissue distribution of total radioactivity in the rat following oral administration of [14C]-AZD6244 (Quantitative whole-body autoradiography) (Study KMR002)
L261: Plasma protein binding of [14C]-selumetinib was not dependent on concentration but varied between species: minipig (93.7%)&lt;dog (94.6%)&lt;monkey (97.7)&lt;human (98.4%) &lt;mouse (98.9%)&lt;rat (99.7%) (Study KPJ003). Plasma:whole blood ratios ranged from 1.9 in rat, 1.3 to 1.4 in dog, 1.3 to 1.5 in monkey and 1.4 to 1.8 in human, indicative that [14C]-selumetinib was predominantly distributed in plasma.
L262: In pigmented and albino rats, the highest observed concentrations of radioactivity in the majority of tissues were measured at 1 or 8 hours post dose with tissue concentrations generally lower than blood and minimal penetration into the CNS (Study KMR002). Relatively higher concentrations of radioactivity were found in the lung, liver, kidney and adrenal gland at each timepoint. There was no evidence of high affinity melanin-specific binding of radioactivity in the pigmented tissues (pigmented skin and uveal tract). Levels of radioactivity in the uveal tract decreased over the course of the study and were not distinguishable from background at 72 hours post dose. The half-life of elimination of radioactivity from the eye (calculated from liquid scintillation counting data) was ca. 60 hours.

L270: 1 Two separate pools of human hepatocytes were used in this study. Peaks with an area less than 2% of the chromatogram radioactivity at all time points are not included. Metabolites without an 'M' label were not observed in human ADME study.
L271: Labels correspond to study annotation and human ADME (YAU169) designation in parenthesis.
L272: ADME Absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion; hADME Human absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion.
L273: In vitro experiments indicate that selumetinib undergoes cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzyme mediated Phase I metabolic oxidation and extensive UDP-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) enzyme mediated Phase II conjugation to form several glucuronide conjugates.
L274: The radiolabeled [14C]-selumetinib human absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion (ADME) study indicated that glucuronidation plays a significant role in the clearance (CL) of selumetinib, with 37% of total radioactivity in plasma assigned to glucuronide conjugates, 12% to residual CYP metabolites (which were not further metabolized through conjugation) and 40% as unchanged selumetinib.
L275: The most abundant circulating metabolite in human (M2), was postulated to result from loss of the ethanediol moiety in combination with glucuronidation and an additional loss of 2 mass units. Full structural characterization of M2 confirmed that selumetinib had undergone cyclization to an imidazoindazole (M10), followed by glucuronidation. A further 6 metabolites were identified in human plasma, each accounting for between 2% and 7% of the circulating drug-related material (mean of all subjects), including metabolites resulting from N-demethylation in combination with the loss of the ethanediol moiety, glucuronidation and intramolecular cyclisation (M1, 3.5%), N-demethylation in combination with glucuronidation (M3, 2.5%), direct glucuronidation of selumetinib (M4, 7.1%), N-demethylation (M8; 2.9%), N-demethylation in combination with oxidation of the primary alcohol group to a carboxylic acid (M11, 3.6%) and a carboxylic acid resulting from hydrolysis of the amide group (M15; AZ13326637, 2.2%).
L276: ## Excretion
L277: *The disposition of [14C]-AZD6244 in the rat following oral and intravenous administration (Study KMR006)*
L278: Following oral administration, the main route of excretion was via the feces, with mean recoveries of 91.8% and 101.6%, in males and females, respectively. Urinary excretion was 9.3% and 4.9%, respectively. Excretion of radioactivity was ca. 98% and 104% by 48 hours respectively.
L279: ## Toxicokinetic data from general toxicology studies
L280: 1-month and 6-month repeat dose studies in mouse (Study 0355AM and Study VKS/0211)
In 1-month study doses of ca. 11, 34 and 103 (reduced to 69) mg/kg BID gave C_{max} and AUC_{(0-12)} values that were slightly greater than dose proportional with no accumulation across the study period. Exposure to selumetinib was similar in male and female mice. Male mice showed higher plasma concentrations of N-desmethyl selumetinib (C_{max} 1.7, 6.0, 7.4 \mug/mL) than female (C_{max} 0.8, 2.4, 4.5 \mug/mL) mice on Day 1 across the dose range but this difference had reduced by Day 28 when N-desmethyl selumetinib AUC_{(0-12)} values were lower in comparison with Day 1.
In 28-week study, mean selumetinib exposure was higher in female mice than in males at Weeks 1 and 4 and the difference was proportionately greater at higher dose levels. At most (Week 4 in 20 mg/kg) exposure was 2-fold higher in females than males. However, no accumulation occurred across the study period and the terminal elimination half-life of selumetinib was between 3.0 and 7.6 hours with no evidence of a sex-difference or change with duration of dosing. Exposure to N-desmethyl selumetinib was higher in male mice than in females. N-desmethyl selumetinib C_{max} and AUC_{(0-12)} values were approximately 3% to 20% and 3% to 28% respectively of those of selumetinib in all dose groups. N-desmethyl selumetinib accounted for a lower proportion to selumetinib at 4 and 16/26 Weeks compared to Day 1. Half-life values for N-desmethyl selumetinib were between 2.9 and 7.1 hours with no evidence that the half-life for N-desmethyl selumetinib was longer than that for selumetinib.

Toxicokinetic data from reproductive toxicology studies
Oral Dose Range Finding Embryofetal Development Study in the Mouse (Study 0430RM)
A Twice Daily Oral (Gavage) Developmental and Perinatal/Postnatal Reproduction Study in the Mouse, Including a Postnatal Behavioral/Functional Evaluation (Study 20082140)
Study 0430RM - Selumetinib C_{max} and area under the plasma concentration-time curve (AUC) values showed an approximately proportional increase with increasing dose across the entire dose range (2.2 to 79.2 \mug/mL and 10.3 to 576.7 \mug.h/mL respectively). For N-desmethyl selumetinib there was a less than proportional increase (0.1 to 0.7 \mug/mL and 0.5 to 5.3 \mug.h/mL respectively).
Study 20082140 - Mean plasma C_{max} levels of selumetinib and N-desmethyl selumetinib at 2 hours post dose on Day 4 in F0 generation mice ranged from 0.45 (1 mg/kg/day) to 4.17 \mug/mL (15 mg/kg/day) and 0.14 (1 mg/kg/day) to 1.45 \mug/mL (15 mg/kg/day) respectively across the dose range. In F1 generation mice the mean plasma levels of selumetinib and N-desmethyl selumetinib at 2 hours post dose on Day 4 ranged from 0.07 (4 mg/kg/day) to 0.36 \mug/mL (15 mg/kg/day) and 0.007 (1 mg/kg/day) to 0.11 \mug/mL (15 mg/kg/day) respectively across the dose range.

L288: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L289: Selumetinib shows improved absorption when administered as the hyd-sulfate salt with approximately dose proportional increase in exposure and no accumulation on repeated administration and moderate half-life in animals. The metabolism is well understood, showing that the most abundant circulating metabolite is an O-glucuronide conjugate of an imidazoindazole derived from selumetinib with loss of the side chain. Metabolism has been demonstrated to involve multiple pathways and enzymes including an appreciable level of glucuronidation. The active N-desmethyl metabolite, M8, was observed in both mouse and human together with several other metabolites. The majority of the drug was excreted in the feces as metabolites; only minor levels of unchanged selumetinib were excreted in the urine or feces.
L290: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L291: FDA confirmed the Applicant's representation of the ADME data based on the submitted reports and generally agrees with the presentation of the findings. In the pigmented rat tissue distribution study (KMR002), organs with relatively high radioactive exposure levels (levels similar to those in the organs listed as targets by the Applicant of liver, lung, kidney, and adrenal gland) included the heart, bladder wall, pancreas, and pituitary and thyroid glands). Consistent with oral administration, the highest, but transient, levels of radioactivity were present in the stomach.
L292: In addition, the available data show that the active N-desmethyl selumetinib metabolite is present in mice and, based on the general toxicology data below, is present at high enough levels to cover the exposure of this metabolite in humans at the 25 mg/m2 twice daily dose. The Applicant also conducted both an Ames assay and a hERG assay to further assess the safety of N-desmethyl selumetinib (negative predictions in both assays). Thus, there is adequate nonclinical data to support the safety of this metabolite. There are no unique human metabolites of selumetinib or metabolites which require additional toxicological coverage. No additional nonclinical ADME studies are warranted.
L293: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L294: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L295: ## The Applicant's Position:
L296: Two forms of selumetinib have been used in the nonclinical development studies. Initial studies were conducted using selumetinib free base, which was later replaced with selumetinib hyd-sulfate. The limited solubility of selumetinib free base resulted in a less than proportional increase in exposure and decreasing bioavailability with increasing dose in the nonclinical studies. The hyd-sulfate form demonstrated enhanced and dose-related increases in exposure in the nonclinical studies compared to free base. Only repeat dose studies conducted with selumetinib hyd-sulfate are included in this document as the toxicities observed in rodents and monkeys with selumetinib free base and selumetinib hyd-sulfate were relatively similar. Additionally, selumetinib hyd-sulfate is the clinically relevant form.
L297: Nonclinical safety of selumetinib has been evaluated in pharmacologically relevant rodent (rats and mice) and non-rodent (cynomolgus monkeys) species. The minor (&lt;10% of the total circulating drug product) human Phase I metabolite, N-desmethyl selumetinib (AZ12442942), is a pharmacologically active metabolite which is not produced to any significant extent in rats and monkeys, but is produced in CD-1 mice therefore, mouse was selected as the rodent
L298: species for the 1-month, 26-week, and reproductive toxicology studies with selumetinib hyd-sulfate.
L299: The nonclinical safety of selumetinib has been evaluated in repeat dose oral toxicity studies of up to 3 months in rats and up to 26 weeks in mice and monkeys; a battery of in vitro and in vivo genetic toxicity and carcinogenicity studies; reproductive toxicology studies and an in vitro phototoxicity assay. Potential genotoxicity of N-desmethyl selumetinib was also evaluated. The reversibility of selumetinib-related findings was evaluated in 1-month studies in mice and monkeys as well as in the 26-week study in mice.
L300: All pivotal toxicology studies were appropriately designed in accordance with ICH guidelines and were conducted in compliance with GLP regulations.
L301: ## **Study Title/Number: 26-week Oral Toxicity Study in the Mouse with Assessment of Effects on Male Fertility Followed by a 13 Week Recovery Period (VKS/0211)**
L302: ## **Key Study Findings:**
L303: - Early termination of 14 males at 20 mg/kg BID group was associated with inflammation and obstruction of the urinary tract.
- Gastrointestinal (GI) tract was identified as the major target organ in mice. The inflammatory and erosive/ulcerative changes 20 mg/kg BID group animals were associated with early decedents in several animals. Inflammatory cell infiltration of the portal area in the liver, reticulocytosis, splenic extramedullary hematopoiesis (EMH), and changes in hematological parameters were noted and considered secondary to erosive and inflammatory changes in the GI tract in mice.
- Tissue mineralization was restricted to minimal multifocal mineralization in the liver, in only a small number of animals at 20 mg/kg BID.
