L1573: ## Additional Efficacy Considerations
L1574: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1575: ## *SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 subgroup analyses*
L1576: The FDA conducted additional analyses of ORR as assessed by the ICR for age, PN status, and PN classification at enrollment. The ORR (95% CI) in patients age &gt;12 years was 29% (10%, 56%), and in patients age ≤ 12 years, the ORR was 52% (34%, 69%). The ORR (95% CI) in patients with progressive disease at enrollment was 57% (34%, 78%), whereas the ORR was 40% (16%, 68%) in patients with non-progressive disease.
L1577: ## *Supportive ORR and DOR data*
L1578: ORR and DOR was assessed for the 24 patients with NF1 PN enrolled in SPRINT Phase 1 and for the 25 patients enrolled in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 2. The NCI central review of ORR for these cohorts was 67% (16/24) (95% CI: 45, 84) and 68% (17/25) (95% CI: 47, 85) respectively. All of
L1579: the 16 responders (100%) in SPRINT Phase 1 and 12 of the 17 responders (71%) in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 2 had a duration of response \ge12 months from the onset of response.
L1580: ## *Descriptive analyses of clinical outcomes data*
L1581: The FDA additionally reviewed clinical outcomes data for the 33 patients in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 who had an objective response per NCI review using REiNS criteria. These data were considered part of the overall benefit assessment for selumetinib in NF1 PN.
L1582: NF1 PN is a tumor that presents in various locations, and tumor-related morbidity varies across patients as it is dependent in part on compression of surrounding normal structures. The SPRINT protocol therefore included multiple COA tools for supportive efficacy assessments due to the diverse tumor geography (e.g., PFTs and sleep studies for tumors near the airway, vision testing for orbital tumors, ROM and strength testing for extremity tumors, etc.). Given the rarity of this condition and the heterogeneity with regard to PN-related morbidity, the sample sizes for each morbidity category were small. These were exploratory endpoints intended to better characterize the clinical benefit of selumetinib in NF1 PN.
L1583: SPRINT Investigators assigned specific target PN-related morbidities to each patient at enrollment, and the clinical outcome measure related to the assigned morbidity was evaluated routinely with radiographic tumor volume assessments. The objective was to show that a reduction in tumor volume was reliably accompanied by a detectable effect on physical function or PN-related symptoms indicating that the patient's quality of life was improved. Given the benign nature of the tumor, this correlation was considered important in the overall benefit:risk assessment for selumetinib.
L1584: The Applicant submitted an IPR document (see Section 8.1.1, Additional Analyses) for each patient enrolled in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1. The template content for the IPRs was agreed upon at the preNDA meeting. Table 39 summarizes the FDA review of tumor volume reduction in the context of supportive clinical outcomes data and important safety information for patients who experienced an objective response.

L1609: ## *IPR Data: FDA Assessment*
L1610: Review of the IPRs allowed for a better characterization of the patient population, the types of functional impairments and symptoms patients were experiencing at enrollment, and potential selumetinib effects on clinical outcomes at the patient level.
L1611: Patients had substantial baseline morbidity related to PN. Many tumors were large and disfiguring, and tumors that were located in critical areas such as near the airway or orbit, even when relatively small in size, caused considerable functional morbidity for patients.
L1612: Among the patients who experienced an objective response per REiNS criteria, 88% (29/33) remained on selumetinib at DCO and appeared to have good tolerability. One additional patient, who discontinued selumetinib due to diagnosis of MPNST, resumed selumetinib under the expanded access program after completing MPNST treatment. Two patients who had an objective response discontinued for PD at Cycles 21 and 31 respectively. One patient discontinued due to adverse events.
L1613: There was further reduction in tumor volume after initial onset of response, with best overall response occurring later for most patients, often after Cycle 13.
L1614: There was a trend of tumor volume reduction associated with at least one improved clinical outcome in most patients who experienced a durable objective response during selumetinib treatment.
L1615: There was evidence of clear benefit in some cases (e.g., tracheostomy tube removal, FEV1 normalization, ROM increase with examples of associated functional improvements, increased bowel/bladder continence, pain decrease). However, in other patients, objective measures were not consistent with patient and/or parent reports of improvement or with tumor volume reduction timepoints.
L1616: Tumor-related disfigurement was present in 88% of patients. Changes in disfigurement were marginal as demonstrated in the photos provided for most patients. Any obvious improvement is noted in the table. However, patients and parents often reported improvement in tumor appearance as part of the GIC that were not obvious to the reviewer. Further, some patients reported changes in texture of the tumor or skin overlying the tumor that they considered important improvements but that were not evident on photos.
L1617: All SPRINT investigators were interviewed prior to submission of the NDA and asked to provide an assessment regarding the overall benefit of selumetinib for their patients. Among the 50 patients, 70% (35/50) were considered to have experienced clinical benefit based on the investigator's assessment. Of the 15 patients who were considered to not have experienced benefit, 5 had an objective response per REiNS criteria; investigators stated that there was
L1618: minimal improvement in clinical morbidity or the toxicities outweighed the benefits in these patients. Among the 17 patients who did not experience a radiographic objective response to selumetinib, 3 patients were considered by investigators to have had benefit with selumetinib treatment based on improved pain symptoms (n=2) and improved bladder continence (n=1).
L1619: ## 8.1.7. Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
L1620: ## **Data:**
L1621: See the Applicant's Position below.
L1622: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1623: Data from the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 study, supported by the SPRINT Phase I and Natural History studies, provide substantial evidence of effectiveness of selumetinib for the treatment of pediatric patients with NF1 and inoperable PN.
L1624: ## **Objective Response**
L1625: - Selumetinib demonstrated a confirmed ORR by NCI POB central analysis of 66.0% (95% CI: 51.2, 78.8), based on 33 responders from 50 evaluable patients in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1. A further 4 (8.0%) patients had an unconfirmed PR. No patients had progression as their best response.
- Reduction in target PN volume was observed early, with almost all patients having a reduction in target PN volume by 4 cycles (i.e., pre-Cycle 5, median percentage change in target PN volume from baseline was -14.31% at this time point).
- High ORR irrespective of PN status at enrolment (progressive PN: ORR of 61.9%) suggesting that PN status at enrolment appears to be independent from response rate and, therefore, not predictive of selumetinib efficacy.
- Inclusion of all available data (regardless of schedule or treatment discontinuation) did not affect ORR (66.0%).
- Supportive ORR data from SPRINT Phase I (66.7% [16 of 24 patients]) confirmed the high ORR observed in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1.
L1626: ## **Duration of Response**
L1627: - Responses occurred early and were durable.Of the 33 responders in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, almost half (14 [42.4%] patients) had achieved a response (\ge 20\% reduction in target PN volume from baseline) by 4 cycles (ie, pre-Cycle 5). In addition, by 8 cycles (ie, pre-Cycle 9), 72.7% of patients had achieved a response.
- The median DoR from onset of response based on NCI POB central analysis was not reached in either the pivotal SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 or supportive Phase I data.
L1628: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213756
## Koselugo (selumetinib)
L1629: - Of the 33 responders in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, the probability of remaining in response after 12 cycles, estimated using the Kaplan-Meier method, was 100%.
- Of the 16 responders in SPRINT Phase I, the estimated proportion of patients remaining in response after 52 cycles, estimated using the Kaplan-Meier method, was 100%.
L1630: ## Progression-free Survival
L1631: - Very few patients progressed in either SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 (3 patients) or SPRINT Phase I (2 patients).Median PFS was not reached in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1. There was a very high probability (94.7% [95% CI: 80.6, 98.7]) of remaining without progressive disease after 2 years in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1.
- Median PFS was not reached in SPRINT Phase I.
L1632: ## Clinical Outcome Assessments
L1633: - Most patients experienced improvements in their COA scores as well as reduced target PN volume.The mean reduction in pain intensity (2.07) achieved by pre-Cycle 13 was considered clinically meaningful. Pain palliation occurred early, within 2 to 4 months, for those patients experiencing pain at baseline (NRS-11 score \ge2) and was sustained over time. Additionally, patients and parents reported a reduction in pain interference over time.
L1634: ## Correlation of Changes in COA and Target PN Volume
L1635: - Strong correlations (r=0.76 [pre-Cycle 9] and 0.71 [pre-Cycle 25]) were detected between NRS-11 scores (patient score \ge2 at baseline) and percentage change in target PN volume. Based on the longitudinal analyses (after adjusting for age, number of morbidities and the baseline value of the clinical outcome), there was evidence of a meaningful correlation between percentage change from baseline in target PN volume and change in NRS-11 score (p&lt;0.001).
- Weak correlations were detected between percentage change in PN volume and PII, PROMIS transformed scores, and ROM. Correlation analyses were impacted by the presence of patients who did not have the potential to improve by the recommended clinically meaningful threshold. Based on the longitudinal analyses (after adjusting for age, number of morbidities and the baseline value of the clinical outcome), a signal was also observed for parent-report PedsQL total (p=0.033).
L1636: ## External Control Data
L1637: Comparison with the external control data confirmed the clinical benefit observed with selumetinib in pediatric patients with NF1 and symptomatic, inoperable PN:
L1638: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213756
## Koselugo (selumetinib)
L1639: - Natural History study data indicate that the PN shrinkage observed in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 can be attributed to selumetinib, rather than the natural course of the disease. The adjusted mean annual growth rate was +21.3% (95% CI: 15.9, 26.8) per year in the Natural History study age-matched cohort compared with -16.9% (95% CI: -20.2, -13.5) per year in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1.
- The PFS benefit of selumetinib treatment observed in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 was confirmed by the Natural History age-matched cohort as most patients in this cohort had progressed (76.1%), median PFS was 1.3 years (95% CI: 1.1, 1.6) with the probability of remaining without progressive disease after 2 years of 30.4% (95% CI: 21.0, 40.3).
- The PFS benefit of selumetinib treatment in observed in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 was confirmed by the placebo arm of tipifarnib Study 01-C-0222, (patients with progressive PN) where most patients in this control group had progressed (23 of 29 patients, 79.3%), median PFS was estimated as 1.0 year, with the probability of remaining without progressive disease after 2 years of 20.6% (95% CI: 7.7, 37.8).
L1640: In conclusion, selumetinib represents a substantial improvement over current clinical management options on clinically significant endpoints and would represent a clear clinical benefit for these patients with NF1 who have a high unmet need as there are no approved treatment options.
L1641: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1642: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's summary of response rate and DOR data from SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and the consistency of responses across relevant subgroups of patients (e.g., baseline progressive or nonprogressive disease). The FDA reiterates that data from the NCI POB NF1 Natural History Study and the placebo arm of Tipifarnib Study 01-C-0222 was not independently confirmed. Given the heterogeneity in the patient population in measured and unmeasured factors (e.g., disease characteristics, patient characteristics, treatment characteristics, etc.), the FDA considered results from the external control studies to be supportive but inadequate for any comparative analyses.
L1643: FDA has the following additional summary comments:
L1644: SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 enrolled 50 pediatric patients with NF1 and an inoperable PN that resulted in significant clinical morbidity. The ORR per NCI central review using REINS criteria was 66% (33/50; 95% CI: 51, 79). All responses were confirmed PRs. Among the 33 responders, the median DOR was not reached, and the proportion of patients who experienced DOR \ge12 months was 82% (27/33). The ORR per ICR was 44% (22/50; 95% CI: 30, 59); the proportion of patients who experienced DOR \ge12 months per ICR was 59% (13/22). The FDA notes that the difference in ORR observed between the NCI central review and the ICR is in part due to tumor volume measurements for 6 patients that showed PN shrinkage between -19.2% and -19.9% as best objective response (i.e., not meeting the 20% threshold for PR).
L1645: Supportive response data was submitted for the 24 patients with NF1 PN enrolled in SPRINT Phase 1 and for 25 patients enrolled in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 2. The NCI central review of ORR for these cohorts was 67% (16/24; 95% CI: 45, 84) and 68% (17/25; 95% CI: 47, 85) respectively. All 16 of the 16 responders (100%) in SPRINT Phase 1 trial and 12 of the 17 responders (71%) in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 2 had a duration of response \ge12 months from the onset of response. These data support the efficacy of selumetinib in the intended indication.
L1646: The response rate results for SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 were considered in the context of clinical outcomes for patients during treatment including the effects on PN-related symptoms and functional impairments. Acknowledging the limitations of the single arm design and the small sample sizes of patients assessed for each PN-related morbidity subset, there was a general trend of tumor volume reduction that was associated with at least one improved clinical outcome in most patients who experienced a durable response during selumetinib treatment. The effect on the associated morbidity appeared to be similarly durable. See Section 8.1.6, Additional Efficacy Considerations.
L1647: ## 8.2. Review of Safety
L1648: ## 8.2.1. Safety Review Approach
L1649: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1650: The principal source of data supporting the safety evaluation of selumetinib in the proposed indication is based on an analysis of the Phase II Stratum 1 data of the SPRINT study. Supportive safety data from the Phase I data of the SPRINT study is presented individually and also pooled with the Phase II Stratum 1 data. The pediatric safety data populations are as follows:
L1651: - **Pivotal pediatric safety data (Phase II Stratum 1 of SPRINT):** 50 pediatric patients aged \ge3 and \le18 years (able to swallow whole capsules) with NF1 and inoperable PN and PN related morbidity at enrolment who received at least 1 dose of selumetinib 25 mg/m2 BID based on BSA. This safety population informs adverse drug reactions (ADRs) in the proposed label.
- **Supportive pediatric safety data:**Phase I of SPRINT: 24 pediatric patients aged \ge3 and \le18 years (able to swallow whole capsules) with NF1 and inoperable PN and received at least 1 dose of selumetinib (20, 25 or 30 mg/m2 BID based on BSA). This study is used to provide further safety information towards the evaluation of the safety profile of selumetinib in pediatric patients.
- Pediatric safety pool: 74 pediatric patients who received selumetinib (all doses included) in the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and Phase I studies. This pool is used
L1652: to provide a greater population size for the characterization of the selumetinib safety and tolerability profile, and to ensure that safety events were evaluated in all available data from selumetinib treated pediatric patients. A subset of this pool was for patients who received selumetinib 25 mg/m2 BID per initial dose assignment in either phase (N=56).
L1653: Further support for the evaluation of the safety of selumetinib as a monotherapy treatment is provided by pooled safety data from adult patients with cancer treated with selumetinib monotherapy in 7 completed AstraZeneca-sponsored studies. Non AstraZeneca-sponsored studies and AstraZeneca sponsored studies of selumetinib in combination with other anti-cancer agents are excluded from the selumetinib Adult monotherapy pool. The safety profile of selumetinib monotherapy in adult patients has been well characterized across multiple studies and is in line with the established safety profile of the MEK inhibitor class effects, including those seen in currently approved MEK inhibitors trametinib, cobimetinib and binimetinib.
L1654: ## • **Supportive adult safety data**
L1655: - Adult monotherapy pool: 347 adult patients in which 79 patients received selumetinib capsules (hyd-sulfate) and 268 patients received selumetinib free base. These data supplement the pivotal and supportive pediatric data and provide a larger overall population for a robust evaluation and characterization of the safety and tolerability profile of selumetinib.
L1656: The pediatric safety data have not been pooled with safety data from AstraZeneca-sponsored studies of selumetinib monotherapy in adult cancer patients due to differences in patient populations, dose levels and exposure.
L1657: Additional supportive data are provided from the safety populations of the following studies for the evaluation of potential and identified risks:
L1658: - 12 Phase I Clinical Pharmacology studies (approximately 380 healthy adult volunteers)
- Ongoing Early Access Program (EAP; [REDACTED](b)(4)/D1346R00002, DCO: 31 January 2019): 166 patients aged 3 years and above at enrolment with NF1-related PN.
- Pediatric externally-sponsored research (ESR) studies (DCO: 31 January 2019). Serious adverse events (SAEs) are reported for 291 pediatric patients who have enrolled in externally-sponsored studies evaluating selumetinib in a range of indications.
L1659: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1660: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's description of the primary and supportive safety datasets included in the NDA. The FDA has the following additional comments regarding the approach to the safety review:
L1661: The pediatric safety pool included 74 patients with NF1 and PN treated with single-agent
L1662: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213756
## Koselugo (selumetinib)
L1663: selumetinib administered at doses ranging from 20 mg/m2 to 30 mg/m2 twice daily during the SPRINT trial. The on-treatment safety monitoring periods for the SPRINT trial were from the time of first dose to 30 days after the last dose of selumetinib. The data cut-off date for the safety results from SPRINT was June 29, 2018. Most of the patients in SPRINT (N=56) received the intended marketing dose of 25 mg/m2 twice daily. Since the study populations and the AE profiles were similar between the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 dataset (N=50) and the pediatric pool (N=74), the FDA focused on the larger pediatric pool for key safety analyses that would inform the warnings and precautions included in the product label.
L1664: The FDA independently reviewed exposure and safety data from SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 (N=50) and from the pediatric pool (N=74). The FDA did not analyze exposure and AE rates by dose cohorts in SPRINT Phase 1 (20 mg/m2, 25mg/m2, 30 mg/m2) due to the small sample sizes of the subgroups receiving doses higher than (N=6), or lower than (N=12), the recommended dose in the label. AE rates in these dosing subgroups were not considered to be informative of the overall safety profile of selumetinib in the study population. It will be noted in the review if important AEs that are possibly dose-related occurred at a dose higher than the labeled dose.
L1665: Given the limited sample size of the pediatric pool (N=74), the FDA additionally reviewed safety data from the “adult monotherapy pool” of 347 adult patients who received single agent selumetinib across seven adult oncology trials. During the review, the FDA noted that the safety profiles of the two selumetinib formulations represented in this pool [hyd-sulfate capsule (N=79); free base oral suspension (N=268)] were different. Patients receiving the capsule formulation had a greater incidence rate of SAEs (33% vs. 24%), AEs \ge Grade 3 (60% vs. 43%) and discontinuations due to AEs (29% vs. 10%) than those who received the free base formulation. The FDA clinical pharmacology team reviewed data comparing the relative bioavailability between the two formulations in adult patients with advanced cancers enrolled in Study D1532C00005. PK parameters of AUC and Cmax were compared and showed that the capsule has ~2-fold AUC and 2.5-fold Cmax as compared to that of the free base. Since the two formulations are not bioequivalent and the capsule formulation is intended for marketing and exhibited more than 2-fold increased drug exposure in plasma, the FDA focused on the cohort of 79 adult patients who received the hyd-sulfate capsule for supportive safety data. The larger safety database was used to identify rare adverse drug reactions that may not have been identified in the smaller dataset. Additionally, the FDA requested and reviewed safety analyses of the Applicant’s global database of selumetinib monotherapy and combination studies for specific MEK inhibitor class safety signals.
L1666: Regarding the data from the ongoing selumetinib expanded access program for NF1 PN (N=166 as of January 2019) and from non-AstraZeneca sponsored pediatric studies of selumetinib in multiple tumor types (N=291 as of January 2019), the FDA notes that determining true AE incidence rates from analysis of these datasets is not possible due to variable reporting requirements between countries (for the expanded access program), data collection methods,
L1667: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213756
## Koselugo (selumetinib)
L1668: and continued accrual in ongoing studies. Further, these datasets had missing elements for a substantial number of the events such as AE duration, toxicity grade, action taken in response to the event, and outcomes. The FDA reviewed the SAE datasets for these studies to further assess known, but less common, safety signals associated with the MEK inhibitor drug class and to identify any new safety signals that may not have been observed in the SPRINT pediatric pool.
L1669: The FDA agreed with the Applicant's plan to not pool the adult and pediatric data given the differences in dose (the pediatric MTD is approximately 60% of the adult MTD), exposure (the median exposure time to selumetinib was much longer in the pediatric data relative to the adult data), and study populations (pediatric patients with underlying NF1 and a benign tumor versus adult patients with refractory cancer diagnoses).
L1670: An updated safety analysis with corresponding datasets (data cut-off date of March 29, 2019), for SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 to supplement the SCS as part of the 90-day safety update was submitted on December 12, 2019. The safety update report included an additional 9 months of exposure for this cohort. Key safety analyses from the updated dataset were verified by the reviewer for the purpose of assessing safety with longer exposures to selumetinib. The 90-day safety update did not identify new safety signals not already discussed in this review.
L1671: ## 8.2.2. Review of the Safety Database
L1672: ## Overall Exposure
L1673: ## **Data:**
L1674: Exposure for the safety populations is presented for the safety analysis set which includes all patients who received at least 1 dose of selumetinib.
L1675: In the pivotal SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, median total exposure to selumetinib was 2.2 years (801.5 days [26.3 months], range 28 to 1053 days). Median actual exposure of 2.0 years (721.5 days [23.7 months], 26 to 1022 days) was similar to median total exposure indicating selumetinib dose interruptions were short for at least half of the patients enrolled in the study. Most patients in the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 received \ge 24 to \le 36 months (66.0% patients) total treatment with selumetinib, the remaining patients received \ge 12 to \le 24 months (22.0% patients) or received &lt; 12 months (6.0%) total treatment with selumetinib.
L1676: The median and duration of exposure in the Pediatric pool (855.5 days, range 28 to 2169 days) was higher than in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 data as it was influenced by the longer exposure to selumetinib in the SPRINT Phase I data (median total treatment duration of approximately 4.4 years [1606 days]). Most patients in the supportive SPRINT Phase I data had received \ge 48 to \le 60 months (42.0% patients) treatment with selumetinib, with 3 patients (12.5%) receiving &gt; 60 months of selumetinib treatment.
L1677: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213756
## Koselugo (selumetinib)
L1678: Longer exposure to selumetinib in the supportive SPRINT Phase I data contributes information towards the safety profile over time.

L1703: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1704: The overall extent and duration of exposure in the pivotal SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 data, supported by the Phase I data and Pediatric pool is considered sufficient to adequately assess the safety profile of selumetinib for the proposed indication, given that selumetinib has been extensively studied in adult patients.
L1705: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1706: The FDA agrees with the exposure data presented in Table 40 and Table 41 for SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 (N=50), the pediatric pool (N=74), the adult hyd-sulfate pool (N=79) and the adult monotherapy pool (n=347).
L1707: The pediatric exposure data demonstrate a relatively long duration of treatment with a median actual exposure time of 25.5 months and nearly 60% of patients receiving selumetinib for more than two years at DCO. This is partly due to the benign condition that was studied and the potential need for chronic dosing for sustained effect given the observed regrowth of tumors during dose interruptions. Although the pediatric pool includes a relatively small sample size, the FDA considered the exposure data sufficient to assess the general safety profile of selumetinib in the intended population.
L1708: The supportive adult safety data in the application is limited by short selumetinib exposure times. The median actual treatment duration for the 79 patients receiving the relevant hyd-sulfate capsule formulation was 50 days (range 1 to 324 days) with approximately 80% exposed
L1709: ## NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 213756
## Koselugo (selumetinib)
L1710: for less than 3 months. This limitation was considered during the review, and the FDA requested that the Applicant submit additional safety analyses of the selumetinib global database and expanded access program for signals that may not have been identified in the adult data available in the original NDA. The Applicant was able to provide these additional analyses, and these are summarized in relevant sections of the review.
L1711: ## Relevant characteristics of the safety population:
L1712: ## Data:
L1713: See the Applicant's Position below.
L1714: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1715: The demographic and disease characteristics of patients recruited to the pediatric SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and supportive SPRINT Phase I populations were representative of the intended target population to be treated in clinical practice. The entry criteria were designed to include patients relevant to the target population and exclude patients whose participation would have been inappropriate for safety reasons.
L1716: Similarly, the demographic and disease characteristics of the adult patients in studies of cancer where also in line with the entry criteria of these studies. Clearly, the pediatric patients in the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and Phase I populations with NF1 and adult studies in cancer differ in terms of demographic (e.g., age), and baseline characteristics, which should be taken into consideration when comparing the safety data from these populations. Despite the differences, the Adult monotherapy pool is still relevant to support the safety populations from the pediatric SPRINT study as it allows a better and clearer understanding of the safety profile of selumetinib through the identification and delineation of the ADRs associated with selumetinib compared to those events related to other factors related to demographics, baseline characteristics and underlying disease. Given the large differences between the 2 populations (age, underlying disease, medical history, prior therapies etc.), AEs occurring commonly in both populations will give stronger confidence and confirmation of being caused by selumetinib and not due to population-related factors. The presentation of the safety profiles in both populations will also allow a better characterization of selumetinib ADRs, their patterns (incidence, severity, duration etc.), their management and the selumetinib risk mitigation measures and whether a modification of these measures is required in different populations.
L1717: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1718: The FDA refers to Section 8.2.1 for a description of the safety datasets included in the NDA. The FDA agrees that the baseline demographic and disease characteristics of the patients enrolled
L1719: in SPRINT are consistent with the intended population for use. The FDA also agrees with not pooling the pediatric and adult data for reasons already discussed.
L1720: Although the FDA agrees that similar types and frequencies of adverse reactions occurring in both the pediatric and adult populations could increase the confidence in determining selumetinib relatedness, it is important to note that AEs that occurred with relatively increased frequency or severity in the pediatric population as compared to the adult population are not necessarily less likely to be related to selumetinib. Confounding variables include short exposure times in the adult dataset, the relatively small sample size of adult patients receiving the relevant (and more bioavailable) capsule formulation, and potential differences in tolerability based on having an underlying benign or malignant condition. Further, while the adult population may be more vulnerable to developing certain adverse reactions due to age, prior therapy, and underlying cancer, absence of specific safety signals in the pediatric data does not indicate that pediatric patients are not at risk for these adverse reactions.
L1721: ## Adequacy of the safety database:
L1722: ## **Data:**
L1723: See the Applicant's Position below.
L1724: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1725: Overall, the safety data allows for an informed assessment of the safety profile of selumetinib and a judgment of the benefit-risk in pediatric patients aged 3 years and above, with NF1 and symptomatic, inoperable PN.
L1726: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1727: The primary safety data from SPRINT, the supportive adult monotherapy pooled data, the supplemental data from the selumetinib expanded access program and externally-sponsored pediatric selumetinib studies and the additional safety analyses of the Applicant's global selumetinib database submitted during the review allowed for an informed risk assessment of selumetinib in the intended indication. The longer term safety profile of selumetinib in the intended population will require further evaluation in the post-marketing setting.

L1739: ## SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and the Pediatric Pool
L1740: In the Pediatric pool, 73/74 (98.6%) patients had at least 1 AE (Table 42); regardless of severity, AEs were assessed by the investigator as causally-related to selumetinib in 73 (98.6%) patients. Approximately two-thirds of patients (50 [67.6%]) had an AE of Grade \ge 3, and 17 (23.0%) patients experienced an SAE. No patients died due to an AE. AEs resulting in permanent discontinuation of selumetinib (DAEs) were reported in 9 (12.2%) patients, 58 (78.4%) patients had AEs leading to dose interruption, and 24 (32.4%) patients had AEs leading to dose reduction of selumetinib.
L1741: The overall incidence of AEs in each category were similar for both the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and Pediatric pool, however, the incidence of AEs leading to dose reduction were higher in the Pediatric pool than the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 population (32.4% vs. 24.0%, respectively). This can be attributed to the inclusion of the Phase I dose escalation study in the Pediatric pool.

L1752: ## Adult Monotherapy Pool
L1753: In the Adult monotherapy pool, 329 patients (94.8%) had at least 1 AE (Table 43); regardless of severity, AEs were assessed by the investigator as causally-related to selumetinib in 309 patients (89.0%). Approximately half of patients (163 [47.0%]) had an AE of Grade \ge 3, including 98 (28.2%) patients for whom the AE was considered causally related to selumetinib by the investigator. SAEs were reported for 91 (26.2%) patients, including 32 (9.2%) of patients for whom the SAE was considered by the investigator as causally related to selumetinib. Eleven patients (3.2%) died due to an AE (pulmonary embolism [3 patients], respiratory failure [2 patients] and acute kidney injury, cardio respiratory arrest, death, haemoptysis, intestinal perforation, and metastases to meninges [1 patient each]). Three (0.9%) patients died due to an AE considered causally related to selumetinib by the investigator (cardio-respiratory arrest, acute kidney injury, and respiratory failure, 1 patient each). DAEs were reported in 54 patients (15.6%), 94 patients (27.1%) had AEs leading to dose interruption, and 19 (5.5%) patients had AEs leading to dose reduction of selumetinib.
L1754: The overall incidence of AEs in each category were broadly similar for both the hyd-sulfate and free base sub-groups, however, the incidence of AEs of CTCAE Grade \ge 3, SAEs and DAEs were higher in the hyd-sulfate sub-group than the free base sub group:
L1755: - AEs of CTCAE Grade \ge 3: 59.5% for hyd-sulfate sub group vs. 43.3% for free base sub group.
- SAEs: 32.9% for hyd-sulfate sub-group vs. 24.3% for free base sub-group.
- DAEs: 29.1% for hyd-sulfate sub-group vs. 11.6% for free base sub-group.

L1771: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1772: Most of the clinical methods used for assessment of safety and tolerability of selumetinib for labelling purposes were standard methods used in most clinical studies, including the reporting of AEs (irrespective of causality or seriousness), treatment-related AEs as judged by the investigator, AEs resulting in death, AEs resulting in permanent discontinuation, clinical laboratory data (clinical chemistry, haematology, and urinalysis), vital signs, physical examination finding, and ECGs (as clinically indicated). BSA was measured in the SPRINT study as dosing was based on BSA.
L1773: The definitions of AEs and SAEs were broadly equivalent between the NCI-sponsored SPRINT studies and the AstraZeneca-sponsored studies in the selumetinib Adult monotherapy pool and the clinical pharmacology studies. As the SPRINT study is a CTEP sponsored study coordinated by the NCI POB, it is not unexpected that some differences exist in the reporting of safety events between SPRINT and the AstraZeneca-sponsored studies included in the selumetinib Adult monotherapy pool. In SPRINT, CTCAE grade changes are reported as new AEs, whereas in AstraZeneca-sponsored studies CTCAE grade changes are reported under the same AE event, however, this has no impact on the interpretation of safety data as summary tables for interpretation have been produced at the patient level not at the event level. Furthermore, in the SPRINT study, the laboratory values outside of normal range are reported as an AE regardless of clinical significance, whereas in AstraZeneca-sponsored studies they are only reported as AEs if they are associated with clinical symptoms or judged clinically significant by the investigator or met seriousness criteria. Finally, multiple symptoms of a single diagnosis are all reported as separate AEs in the SPRINT study, whereas in AstraZeneca-sponsored studies the diagnosis is reported as an AE. The approach to AE reporting in SPRINT is conservative but advantageous to this small study of 50 patients as it will result in a high volume of AEs reported in the study database and will aid in building the clinical picture of every event.
L1774: In the SPRINT study, safety assessments were generally conducted at the respective investigator sites, however as some enrolled patients in the study lived some distance from the NCI POB, some study mandated evaluations (ie, medical history, physical examinations and laboratory evaluations) were conducted by local health care providers (HCPs). However, for patients enrolled at NCI POB, all LVEF assessments were performed at NCI POB and not at the local HCPs. The impact of the use of local HCPs on AE reporting was assessed by comparing the frequency of expected AEs (based on the selumetinib Investigator's Brochure) reported by the NCI POB site which used local HCPs to sites which did not use local HCPs.
L1775: Adverse events across the SPRINT study data, Pediatric pool and Adult monotherapy pool were classified according to the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) (Version 21.0) and graded according to the NCI's common toxicity criteria (Version 4.03). For those events without assigned CTCAE grades, the recommendation in the CTCAE criteria that converts mild, moderate and severe events into CTCAE grades were used.
L1776: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1777: The FDA agrees with the general methods used for the assessment of safety and tolerability of selumetinib and has verified the summary safety data presented in Table 42 for the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and the pediatric pool cohorts, and the adult summary safety data presented in Table 43.
L1778: The FDA acknowledges the differences in AE reporting between the SPRINT trial (conducted by NCI) and the AstraZeneca-sponsored trials included in the adult safety pool as detailed in the Applicant's position statement and does not consider these differences to substantially impact the ability to reliably assess the safety profile of selumetinib in the intended indication.
L1779: With regard to the portion of safety data collected by local healthcare providers, the Applicant informed the FDA at a Type C meeting on July 18, 2018, that all of the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 patients enrolled at NCI (N=27) had at least one protocol-required safety evaluation performed by their local clinicians. No patient had a tumor-staging visit outside of the NCI POB. The FDA requested that the Applicant conduct and submit subgroup safety analyses for patients who received all study-related evaluations at one of clinical sites and for patients who had non-re-staging visits at local centers. There were no substantial differences between the AE profiles and no indication of AE underreporting for patients who underwent a portion of their safety assessments by local clinicians. See the FDA Assessment under Section 8.2.7.
L1780: The FDA also assessed the adequacy of the Applicant's mapping of AE verbatim terms to MedDRA preferred terms (PTs) for 100% of the AE.xpt dataset for the pediatric pool. Of the 5,144 line listings in this dataset, the reviewer used matching of identical verbatim and MedDRA PTs (n=2,362 line listings) as well as manual evaluation of the remaining verbatim terms (n=2,782 line listings). The majority of nonidentical terms were due to spelling differences (diarrhoea versus diarrhea) similar conditions (e.g., dermatitis acneiform versus acneiform rash), laboratory abnormality abbreviations and descriptions, and verbatim terms that included descriptors (e.g., dry skin with peeling versus dry skin). Overall, the MedDRA PTs listed in the dataset adequately represented the verbatim terms from the CRFs.
L1781: ## **Routine Clinical Tests**
L1782: ## **Data:**
L1783: See the Applicant's Position below.
L1784: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1785: ## **SPRINT**
L1786: The following laboratory parameters were collected for both SPRINT Phase I and SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1:
L1787: - Hematology: Complete blood count with differential and platelets.
L1788: - Clinical chemistry: Electrolytes (including sodium, potassium, chloride, CO2), calcium, phosphorus, magnesium, creatinine, blood urea nitrogen, glucose, AST, ALT, bilirubin, urinalysis, total protein, albumin, creatine phosphokinase (CPK), amylase, and lipase.
L1789: Samples for measurement of clinical laboratory parameters were collected at screening, at intervals throughout the treatment period and during the follow-up period. Any abnormal laboratory values were to be followed until return to baseline or stabilization of event.
L1790: Laboratory assessments were performed by the hospital's local laboratory. If any abnormal laboratory result was considered clinically significant, the investigator was to provide details about the action taken with respect to the test drug and about the patient's outcome.
L1791: For the Pediatric pool, laboratory results were classified according to local reference ranges (ULN or lower limit of normal [LLN], as applicable and assigning low/high flags for haematology and clinical chemistry values). Grades were assigned based on laboratory values and CTCAE version 4.03 requirements only; clinical assessment of the CTCAE grade were not taken into account.
L1792: ## Selumetinib Adult Monotherapy Pool
L1793: Blood tests including standard hematology and clinical chemistry were performed according to the individual CSPs.
L1794: The selumetinib Adult monotherapy pooled analysis included the following laboratory parameters that were collected in all 7 adult monotherapy studies:
L1795: - Hematology: erythrocyte count; leukocyte count; absolute neutrophil count; absolute lymphocyte count; absolute monocyte count; absolute eosinophil count; absolute basophil count; hemoglobin; platelet count.
- Clinical chemistry: albumin; AST; ALT; alkaline phosphatase (ALP); creatinine; gamma glutamyltransferase; magnesium; potassium; sodium; total protein; total bilirubin; urea nitrogen; corrected calcium; calcium x phosphate.
L1796: For the adult monotherapy pool, laboratory results were classified according to the NCI CTCAE criteria Version 4.03 using project reference ranges (ULN or LLN, as applicable). Grades were assigned based on laboratory values and CTCAE requirements only; clinical assessment of the CTCAE grade were not taken into account. CTCAE grades were not defined for total protein, urea nitrogen, absolute eosinophil count, absolute basophil count, or absolute monocyte count.
L1797: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1798: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's description of the routine laboratory assessments collected during SPRINT and the adult clinical trials. The FDA notes that CPK was not routinely assessed in the adult trials as it was during SPRINT and that CPK elevation was a frequent laboratory abnormality and AE observed during SPRINT.
L1799: ## 8.2.4. Safety Results
L1800: ## Deaths
L1801: ## Data:
L1802: ## **Deaths: SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and the Pediatric pool**
L1803: No deaths were reported in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 or SPRINT Phase I.
L1804: ## **Deaths: Adult monotherapy pool**
L1805: A total of 172 (49.6%) deaths occurred in the Adult monotherapy pool, including 152 (43.8%) deaths due to disease progression. The total number of patients who died due to an AE during selumetinib treatment (AEs with an onset date on or after the date of first dose and up to and including 30 days following last dose of selumetinib) was 11 (3.2%), including 3 (0.9%) patients for whom the AE leading to death was considered causally-related to selumetinib (Table 43). The number of patients who died due to an AE that started more than 30 days after permanent discontinuation of selumetinib was 3 (0.9%) (Table 44).
L1806: Overall, AEs with the outcome of death were most commonly reported in the system organ class (SOC) of Respiratory, thoracic and mediastinal disorders. Full details are presented in the individual study CSRs.
L1807: Of the 14 patients who died due to an AE:
L1808: - 11 patients had an AE leading to death during selumetinib treatmentIn 7 (2.0%) patients, the investigator considered the death to be due to both the disease under investigation and a fatal AE.
- In 4 (1.2%) patients, the death was considered to be as a consequence of the AEs alone
- Of these 11 patients, 3 patients, all from the free base sub group, experienced an AE with the outcome of death that was assessed by the investigator as causally related to selumetinib.
- 3 patients had an AE leading to death with an onset date falling after 30 day follow up period.In 2 (0.6%) patients, the investigator considered the death to be due to both the disease under investigation and a fatal AE.
- In 1 (10.3%) patient, the death was considered to be as a consequence of the AEs alone.

L1837: APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL
L1838: 226
L1839: Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
L1840: *Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as "The Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.*
L1841: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1842: No AEs with fatal outcome were reported in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 or SPRINT Phase I.
L1843: There were a number of deaths in the Adult monotherapy pool, but this is expected in a patient population with advanced cancer.
L1844: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1845: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's analysis of deaths. In summary, there were no deaths in the pediatric pool at the time of the NDA submission and no pediatric deaths in the 90-day safety update report. Across the adult safety pool, 4% (14/347) of patients experienced death due to an AE or "other" reason. Three percent (10/347) had a fatal AE within 30 days of receiving selumetinib. These fatal AEs were pulmonary embolus (3), respiratory failure (2), cardiac arrest (1), meningeal metastases (1), death (1), acute kidney injury (1), and hemoptysis (1). The FDA analysis did not include one additional patient who experienced a fatal intestinal perforation because the CSR, as the Applicant has noted above, states that this patient died due to progressive disease (metastatic pancreatic cancer) rather than an AE. Three patients (0.9%) experienced a fatal AE that was at least possibly related to selumetinib: cardiac arrest, acute kidney injury, and respiratory failure. The FDA reviewed the narratives for patients (b) (6) and agrees with the Applicant's comments in Table 45. While selumetinib exposure cannot be ruled out as possibly contributing to these fatal AEs, there were multiple confounding variables in the causality analysis, including underlying disease and medical history.
L1846: The FDA additionally reviewed the narrative for patient (b) (6) with cause of death listed as "death". This patient was listed as having two Grade 5 AEs in the AE database: death and abdominal pain. The event of death was assessed as unrelated to selumetinib, but the event of abdominal pain was assessed as possibly related to selumetinib.
L1847: *Patient* (b) (6): 62 yo male with stage 4 melanoma with abdominal, bone, liver, lymph node, retroperitoneal and peripancreatic metastases, enrolled in Study D1532C00003. Significant medical history included myocardial infarction and placement of a cardiac stent. The patient had undergone resection of primary melanoma on left leg and radiation to the left inguinal and iliac lymph nodes. The patient experienced nausea and abdominal pain while on study requiring interruption. On Day 12, the patient had abdominal pain and abdominal distension. Both events were considered to be related to selumetinib by the investigator, and selumetinib was permanently discontinued due to the event of abdominal pain. A few days later, the patient experienced hematemesis at home but refused to seek medical attention. The patient died at home on Day 17. The death was reported by the emergency doctor; the cause of death was reported as unknown. There were no signs of tumor progression. There was no autopsy. The abdominal pain event was coded as Grade 5 due to the fatal outcome; however the event of unexplained death was considered unrelated to selumetinib.
L1848: **Reviewer:** There is insufficient information in this case to determine selumetinib relatedness. The patient experienced GI toxicity and abdominal pain prior to the event, and both were considered related to study drug, and GI toxicity is a known drug class effect. The patient did not have evidence of progressive disease at the time of death. While the patient had extensive disease at enrollment including abdominal and peripancreatic metastases, selumetinib cannot be ruled out as possibly contributing to this fatal event.
L1849: The FDA additionally reviewed the narrative for patient [REDACTED](b) (6) with cause of death listed as myocarditis and congestive heart failure. This death occurred outside of the 30-day reporting window, but the investigator assessed the event as related to selumetinib.
L1850: Patient [REDACTED](b) (6): 37 yo with advanced melanoma. Medical history was significant for hypercholesterolemia, hypertension, renal transplantation and radiotherapy to the left axilla and supraclavicular fossa. The patient's concomitant medications included: clonidine, ezetimibe, pravastatin and sirolimus/prednisolone for approximately 3 years. Baseline LVEF was approximately 55%. On Day 6 of selumetinib, the patient had Grade 2 dermatitis acneiform leading to drug interruption. Selumetinib was never restarted. On Day 19, the patient experienced Grade 3 abdominal pain and received antibiotics for a possible infection. On Day 28 the patient was diagnosed with myocarditis and on Day 43, the patient developed congestive heart failure and died 5 days later. The myocarditis and congestive heart failure were assessed as related to selumetinib by the investigator. The investigator also thought that there may have been an interaction between selumetinib and sirolimus.
L1851: **Reviewer:** There are multiple confounding variables that prevent a reliable assessment of causality in this case including the advanced underlying disease, long-term immunosuppression, concomitant medications and lack of details regarding the myocarditis diagnosis.

L1871: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1872: ## **Serious Adverse Events: SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and the Pediatric Pool**
L1873: Overall, 12 (24.0%) patients in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, and 17 (23.0%) patients in the Pediatric pool had at least 1 SAE during the treatment period (Table 46), and 8 (16.0%) patients in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and 11 (14.9%) patients in the Pediatric pool, had AEs that led to hospitalisation. The incidence of SAEs considered to be possibly related to selumetinib were similar in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 (6 [12.0%]) and the Pediatric pool (8 [10.8%]) (Table 42).
L1874: The most commonly reported SAEs by PT in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, were anaemia, diarrhoea and hypoxia (2 [4.0%] patients, each), all other SAEs were reported in 1 (2.0%) patient, each. In the Pediatric pool, the most commonly reported SAEs by PT were anaemia, blood CPK increased, diarrhea, hypoxia, and pyrexia (2 [2.7%] patients each), all other SAEs were reported in 1 (1.4%) patient, each (Table 46).
L1875: Of the most frequently reported AEs by PT in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, only the PTs of blood CPK increased (2.0%), diarrhoea (4.0%), and pyrexia (2.0%), had SAEs reported.
L1876: In SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, the majority of SAEs had recovered with either no action taken or following a temporary dose interruption or dose reduction at the DCO date for this analysis. The outcome was unknown for 1 (2.0%) patient with an SAE of humerus fracture, and 4 (8.0%) patients had an SAE that led to permanent discontinuation of selumetinib
L1877: The incidences of the most common SAEs in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and the Pediatric pool were too low for a meaningful comparison with those observed in the Adult monotherapy pool (Table 46 and Table 47). Although the Pediatric pool and the Adult monotherapy pool has similar incidences of SAEs reported (23.0% and 26.2%, respectively), the incidence of SAEs in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 was lower than that of the adult hyd-sulfate sub-group (24.0% vs 39.2%, respectively).
L1878: ## **Serious Adverse Events: Adult Monotherapy Pool**
L1879: Overall, in the Adult monotherapy pool, 91 (26.2%) patients had at least 1 SAE (including fatal and non-fatal SAEs) during the treatment period, and SAEs were reported for 26 (39.2%) patients in the hyd-sulfate sub-group (Table 47). In total, 22 (27.8%) patients in the hyd-sulfate sub-group and 78 (22.5%) patients in the Adult monotherapy pool, had AEs that led to hospitalisation.
L1880: In the hyd-sulfate sub-group, the SAEs by PT reported in \ge 2.0\% of patients were vomiting (4 [5.1%]), lower respiratory tract infection (3 [3.8%]), and anaemia, constipation and pyrexia (2 [2.5%] patients, each). In the Adult monotherapy pool, only the PT of vomiting (8 [2.3%]), was reported as an SAE in \ge 2\% of patients (Table 47). With the exception of lower respiratory tract infection, all other SAEs reported in \ge 2\% of patients are ADRs of selumetinib.
L1881: Whilst the most frequently reported AEs by PT in the hyd-sulfate sub-group and the Adult monotherapy pool were: fatigue, nausea, diarrhoea, dermatitis acneiform and oedema peripheral, the number of patients in whom these PTs were reported as SAEs was low: fatigue 0% vs 0%, nausea 1.3% vs 0.9%, diarrhoea 0 vs 0.9%, dermatitis acneiform 0% vs 0.3%, and oedema peripheral 0% vs 0.3%.
L1882: The incidence of SAEs considered to be possibly related to selumetinib were similar in the hyd-sulfate sub-group (7 [8.9%]) and the Adult monotherapy pool (32 [9.2%]).
L1883: In the Adult monotherapy pool, the majority (64.9%) of SAE events had recovered with either no action taken or following a temporary dose interruption or dose reduction, or were recovering. Selumetinib was permanently discontinued for 16 (4.6%) patients with non-fatal SAEs, and for 10 (2.9%) patients the SAEs were fatal.
L1884: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1885: The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's analysis of SAEs occurring during SPRINT and across the adult safety pool. In summary, among the 74 patients in the pediatric pool, 23% (17/74) experienced at least one SAE, including 11% who experienced a SAE considered at least possibly related to selumetinib. The events were varied; SAEs occurring in \ge 2 patients included diarrhea, CPK elevation, anemia, pyrexia, and hypoxia. Across the adult safety cohort receiving the hyd-sulfate formulation, 33% (26/79) experienced at least one SAE including 10% with SAEs considered related to selumetinib. SAEs that occurred in \ge 2 adult patients included vomiting (4), lower respiratory tract infection (3), anemia (2), constipation (2), and pyrexia (2). Please note that the absolute number of patients experiencing a SAE in the adult hyd-sulfate cohort (26) is correct; however, the "39.2%" result noted in paragraphs 5 and 6 of the Applicant's position statement appears to be a typographical error (32.9% is the correct SAE rate).
L1886: The FDA reviewed narratives for all SAEs occurring in the pediatric pool. Summaries of these narratives are presented here. The FDA was in agreement with the investigators' assessments in most cases and included reviewer comments following the summary when relevant.
L1887: ## **SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 SAEs**
L1888: ## **Patient (b) (6): CPK elevation**
L1889: 16 yo male with left truncal PN. Experienced asymptomatic Grade 2 CPK elevation starting on Day 25. AE became serious when severity increased to Grade 3 on Day 52. Remained Grade 3 and asymptomatic for 58 days, decreased to grade 2 for 56 days and then rose to **Grade 4** on Day 221. Dose was interrupted for 9 days followed by **dose reduction**. Cardiac workup showed CK-MB less than 5% and CPK elevation was considered to be a noncardiac event. Patient discontinued selumetinib on Day 585 for progressive disease. The investigator assessed the CPK elevation as related to selumetinib.
L1890: ## **Patient (b) (6): Anemia, hypoxia, osteomyelitis, skin ulcer**
L1891: 11 yo male with head/neck PN and tracheostomy-dependent at enrollment due to airway compression. Medical history significant for respiratory failure due to infection and ventroperitoneal shunt for hydrocephalus. Skin ulcer SAE led to **permanent discontinuation** of selumetinib. On Day 540, patient was diagnosed with **Grade 4 skin ulcer** in region of previous pilonidal cyst. On Day 543 patient was seen by plastic surgery and orthopaedics and diagnosed with Stage 4 pressure ulcer and Grade 3 osteomyelitis. The patient underwent excision of coccyx bone. The patient had concurrent Grade 1 fever and Grade 3 anemia and received a blood transfusion. A Grade 4 hypoxia event occurred due to mucus plus on Day 544 and resolved with suctioning. The investigator assessed the events of skin ulcer, osteomyelitis and anemia as related to selumetinib and the hypoxia event as unrelated to selumetinib.
L1892: ## **Patient (b) (6) : Bacterial tracheitis, hypoxia, constipation**
L1893: 6 ½ yo male with target PN in head/neck region. Patient was tracheostomy-dependent at enrollment and had medical history significant for diarrhea, pulmonary stenosis and Pierre Robin syndrome. On Day 744, the patient experienced SAEs of Grade 2 bacterial tracheitis (positive MRSA culture) and Grade 3 hypoxia resulting in selumetinib interruption and hospitalization for antibiotics and oxygen support. The investigator considered these SAEs to be unrelated to selumetinib. On Day 778, the patient had Grade 2 constipation and was hospitalized for bowel clean-out. The patient had 7 prior constipation events on study. The investigator assessed the constipation as related to selumetinib and decreased bowel motility. The patient continued on selumetinib without dose modification during constipation.
L1894: ## **Patient (b) (6) : Diarrhea**
L1895: 3 ½ yo male with trunk and upper leg PN who presented on Day 291 with Grade 3 diarrhea requiring hospitalization. The patient had prior episodes of eosinophilic colitis and C. difficile colitis; however, C. difficile and stool cultures were negative at the time of this event. Selumetinib was interrupted for 2 days, and the diarrhea resolved in 8 days. The patient discontinued selumetinib on Day 564 (patient choice). The investigator assessed the diarrhea as related to selumetinib.
L1896: ## **Patient (b) (6) : Acute kidney injury, anemia, blood creatinine increased, hyperkalemia, hyperuricemia, hypocalcemia, proteinuria**
L1897: 11 yo male with PN of neck and trunk who presented on Day 196 with acute renal failure after more than two weeks of mild vomiting, diarrhea and poor oral intake. The patient was admitted with Grade 3 acute kidney injury, Grade 3 anemia, Grade 4 creatinine elevation, Grade 3 hyperkalemia, Grade 3 hypocalcemia and Grade 1 proteinuria. On Day 198, the patient developed Grade 4 hyperuricemia. All events resolved. The patient had interrupted selumetinib on Day 190, and these events led to **permanent discontinuation** of selumetinib. The investigator attributed the acute kidney injury, anemia, hyperkalemia, hyperuricemia, hypocalcemia, and proteinuria to selumetinib and the creatinine elevation to kidney injury.
L1898: **Reviewer:** This patient's kidney injury and electrolyte abnormalities could be partially attributed to dehydration given the prior weeks of vomiting and diarrhea. The selumetinib contribution may have been direct or indirect if selumetinib caused the GI toxicity preventing the patient from maintaining adequate hydration prior to the event.
L1899: ## **Patient (b) (6) : Diarrhea**
L1900: 15 ½ yo male with PN in neck/trunk region. The patient had two Grade 3 SAEs of diarrhea. Selumetinib was interrupted and restarted at a reduced dose after the first event on Day 18 and **permanently discontinued** on Day 64 after the second event of Grade 3 diarrhea.
L1901: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Hypotension**
L1902: 15 ½ yo with neck/trunk PN experienced **Grade 4** hypotension following a tumor debulking surgery. The investigator considered the hypotension to be unrelated to selumetinib and probably related to anesthesia. Other concurrent relevant adverse events reported at the time of the hypotension included sinus tachycardia and ejection fraction decreased.
L1903: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Colitis**
L1904: 6 yo male with left head PN experienced **Grade 3** colitis starting on Day 270 resulting in selumetinib interruption. The colitis was due to a C. difficile infection. The patient resumed selumetinib upon resolution.
L1905: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Influenza, pyrexia**
L1906: 15 ½ yo male with neck/trunk PN experienced **Grade 3** influenza on Day 386. Patient had Grade 2 pyrexia for two days concurrent with influenza. Patient required hospitalization. The investigator assessed the events of influenza and pyrexia as unrelated to selumetinib. The patient resumed selumetinib upon resolution.
L1907: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Hematoma, fracture, skin infection**
L1908: 10 yo male with trunk/extremity PN presented on Day 742 with **Grade 3** hematoma, fracture, and skin infection in the left arm. Medical history was significant for lymphedema in this arm, no sensation, and a recent fall. The patient was hospitalized for the infection and received IV antibiotics. Orthopedics managed the fracture. The investigator assessed the events of hematoma, skin infection and humerus fracture as unrelated to selumetinib.
L1909: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **MPNST**
L1910: 15 yo male with neck/trunk PN experienced malignant transformation of PN to MPNST after one year on selumetinib. The left cervical MPNST was identified during pre-Cycle 13 MRI evaluation. The event resulted in selumetinib **discontinuation**, but the MPNST was not considered related to selumetinib exposure.
L1911: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Urinary tract infection (UTI), lymphopenia, neutropenia**
L1912: 4 yo male with trunk/extremity/pelvis PN and associated bladder dysfunction experienced two **Grade 3** UTIs during selumetinib treatment, both associated with Grade 3 lymphopenia and the first event associated with Grade 3 neutropenia. PMH significant for nephrostomy tubes and UTIs. The investigator assessed these events as unrelated to selumetinib.
L1913: There was one additional SAE that occurred in Patient (b) (6) from SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 reported in the 90-day safety update. The patient was diagnosed with MPNST more than three years after initiation of selumetinib. Malignant transformation of PN to MPNST is a known risk in patients with NF1 PN and would not be considered related to selumetinib.
L1914: ## SPRINT Phase 1 SAEs
L1915: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **CPK elevation**
L1916: 5 yo female with left pelvic PN who presented with asymptomatic **Grade 4** CPK increase on Day 505 resulting in selumetinib interruption and subsequent **dose reduction**. The patient had experienced intermittent Grade 1-2 CPK increases during the study and then a recurrent Grade 3 CPK increase on Day 1098. The patients experienced Grade 1 noncardiac chest pain concurrent with the second Grade 3 CPK increase. These latter events were ongoing at DCO.
L1917: **Reviewer:** *The CSR states that there were no concurrent myalgia or muscle weakness symptoms during the CPK elevation events; however, the concurrent noncardiac chest pain is not well-described, and there is insufficient information to conclude that this pain was not musculoskeletal in origin.*
L1918: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Pyrexia, vulval cellulitis**
L1919: 8 yo female with left neck/chest PN presented with Grade 2 fever and **Grade 3** vulval cellulitis on Day 14. The patient was hospitalized for IV antibiotics, and selumetinib was held. The patient resumed selumetinib at a reduced dose upon resolution. This SAE was counted as a DLT at the 20mg/m2 BID dose level during SPRINT Phase 1. The investigator assessed these events as related to selumetinib.
L1920: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Fracture**
L1921: 9 yo male with pelvis PN experienced a **Grade 3** fracture of right toe Day 139. Selumetinib was interrupted, but the investigator assessed the event as unrelated to study drug.
L1922: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Headache**
L1923: 6 yo male with left retroperitoneal PN presented with **Grade 3** headache on Day 968. Resolved in 2 days. Medical history significant for prior, less severe, headaches. Investigator assessed headache as unrelated to selumetinib.
L1924: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Vomiting, abdominal pain, dehydration**
L1925: 3 yo male with left eye PN, presented with **Grade 3** vomiting followed 2 days later by grade 3 abdominal pain and dehydration requiring hospitalization and selumetinib **interruption**. The investigator assessed these events as unrelated to selumetinib exposure.
L1926: ## SAEs - Pediatric Supportive Data
L1927: The FDA reviewed the SAE datasets for the selumetinib expanded access program in NF1 PN and externally-sponsored pediatric studies of selumetinib (SAEs were only reported from ongoing Study D1532C00043/PBTC-029 in pediatric patients with low grade glioma). As discussed in Section 8.2.1, calculation of AE incidence rates from these data is not possible due to an unreliable denominator. AEs that were considered serious and related to selumetinib and occurred in at least 2 patients with NF1 PN in the expanded access program include stomatitis, rash, paronychia, edema, hypertension, ejection fraction decrease, diarrhea, dermatitis
L1928: acneiform, CPK elevation, AST elevation and abdominal pain. Of these, SAEs that led to discontinuation of selumetinib were LVEF decrease and rash. Across the externally-sponsored pediatric studies of selumetinib, SAEs that were assessed as related to selumetinib and occurred in at least 2 patients included CPK increase, rash, paronychia, stomatitis, vision impairment, optic nerve disorder, vomiting, dehydration, headache, and ALT elevation. Of these, SAEs that led to discontinuation of selumetinib were optic nerve disorder in 2 patients, vision impairment with retinal detachment in one patient, stomatitis, ALT elevation and rash.
L1929: **Reviewer:** *The SAEs that were reported in these supportive pediatric datasets are consistent with the safety profile of selumetinib and the MEK inhibitor drug class. Additional details on the ocular SAEs are provided in Section 8.2.5.*

L1955: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L1956: Seven DAEs of selumetinib were reported for 6 (12.0%) patients of SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1. No AEs leading to permanent discontinuation of selumetinib were reported by more than 1 patient. Most DAEs were considered to be related to selumetinib by the investigator, except for AEs of blood creatinine increased (2 events in 1 patient) and malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumour. The percentage of patients with DAEs was low and all DAEs resolved after selumetinib was stopped. Similar observations and frequencies of DAEs were observed in the Pediatric pool (12 DAEs in 9 patients, 12.2%). Overall 5 DAE events were considered SAEs: skin ulcer, malignant peripheral nerve sheath tumour, acute kidney injury (1 event), creatinine increased (2 events in 1 patient), and diarrhoea.
L1957: The frequently observed AEs (&gt;40\%) that characterize the selumetinib safety profile (acneiform rashes, gastrointestinal events [diarrhoea and vomiting], paronychia and pyrexia) did not generally lead to discontinuation of selumetinib. The low percentage of permanent discontinuation of selumetinib due to AEs in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and the Pediatric pool indicates that selumetinib at the recommended daily dose is tolerated by this pediatric NF1 patient population.
L1958: A similar overall incidence of DAEs was observed in the Adult monotherapy pool (15.6%) was similar, though the majority of DAEs were CTCAE Grade \ge 3 and most DAEs were considered to be related to selumetinib by the investigator.
L1959: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L1960: The FDA agrees with the presentation of AEs leading to discontinuation of selumetinib as presented in Table 48 (SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and Pediatric pool columns) and Table 49. In summary, 12% (9/74) of patients in the pediatric pool experienced at least one AE that resulted in permanent discontinuation of selumetinib. These included diarrhea, paronychia, nausea, stomatitis, fatigue, acute kidney injury, skin ulcer, MPNST, increased weight, myalgia, and gastroesophageal reflux. None of these events occurred in more than one patient. All of the events, with the exception of MPNST and creatinine elevation (attributed to acute kidney injury which was considered possibly related to selumetinib), were assessed as at least possibly related to selumetinib exposure. Five events were considered SAEs: skin ulcer, MPNST, acute kidney injury, creatinine increased, and diarrhea.
L1961: Regarding the supportive adult data, AEs leading to selumetinib discontinuation occurred in 29% (23/79) of the adult hyd-sulfate cohort. Events that occurred in at least two patients included fatigue, dyspnea, AST elevation, and LV dysfunction. One patient experienced a retinal vein occlusion event that led to discontinuation. See Section 8.2.5.5 for a summary of this event.
L1962: The FDA reviewed narratives for all AEs leading to discontinuation of selumetinib in the pediatric pool. Discontinuation AEs that were serious are included in the SAE narrative summaries above. Summaries of the nonserious discontinuation AEs are presented below. The FDA was in agreement with the investigators' assessments and included reviewer comments following the summary when relevant.
L1963: ## **SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1**
L1964: ## **Patient (b) (6) : Weight increased**
L1965: 7 ½ yo with pelvis/extremity experienced **Grade 3** weight gain on Day 104 triggering selumetinib interruption and subsequent dose reduction. Recurrent Grade 3 weight increase on Day 203 led to interruption and a second dose reduction (from 10 mg QHS and 20 mg QAM to
L1966: 10 mg BID). Study treatment was subsequently permanently discontinued on Day 277 due to weight gain. The investigator assessed the weight gain AE as possibly related to selumetinib. Upon later disease progression, the patient went on to restart selumetinib as part of the expanded access program.
L1967: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Paronychia**
L1968: 7 ½ yo with trunk/extremity PN who had multiple episodes of paronychia ranging in severity from Grade 1 to 3 while on selumetinib. The patient had 2 dose reductions and then permanently discontinued selumetinib on Day 449 after a recurrent **Grade 3** paronychia. The patient received multiple treatments for paronychia while on study including bacitracin, cefalexin, clindamycin, gentamicin, sulfamethoxazole/trimethoprim and triamcinolone. The investigator assessed the paronychia AEs as possibly related to selumetinib.
L1969: ## **SPRINT Phase I**
L1970: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Fatigue, myalgia, CPK increase**
L1971: 17 ½ yo male with neck/trunk/pelvis PN who had multiple events of CPK elevation during selumetinib exposure. He had 2 dose reductions for **Grade 3** CPK elevation. The patient reported Grade 1 myalgia during one CPK elevation which resolved without treatment and was considered related to selumetinib and to doing push-ups. The patient subsequently experienced **Grade 2 fatigue and myalgia** on Day 499 and selumetinib was discontinued on Day 500 based on these events. The patient had concurrent Grade 1 CPK increase starting Day 505. The myalgia resolved approximately 6 weeks later. The investigator assessed the myalgia and fatigue as possibly related to selumetinib.
L1972: **Reviewer:** Although the AE leading to discontinuation was coded as fatigue and myalgia, it is relevant that there was CPK elevation noted a few days after these symptoms manifested. This event appears to be a symptomatic CPK elevation.
L1973: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Stomatitis**
L1974: 5 ½ yo female with left orbit PN started at dose level 30 mg/m2 in SPRINT Phase 1. The patient had multiple episodes of stomatitis varying between Grade 1 and 3 in severity during selumetinib exposure. The patients had two dose reductions for stomatitis. A subsequent stomatitis on Day 997 led to interruption followed by discontinuation on Day 1007. The investigator assessed the stomatitis events as related to selumetinib.
L1975: ## **Patient** (b) (6) : **Gastroesophageal reflux disease**
L1976: 13 yo with neck/shoulder PN presented on Day 141 with Grade 2 GERD leading to discontinuation on Day 158. Assessed as related by the investigator.

L2004: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2005: The incidence of AEs leading to dose modification (interruption and/or reduction) was similar in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and the Pediatric pool (80.0% vs 78.4%, respectively). All patients who had a dose modification had an AE leading to dose interruption in both SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 (40 [80.0%]) and the Pediatric pool (58 [78.4%]). The incidence of AE leading to dose reduction of selumetinib was 24.0% (12 patients) in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and 32.4% (24 patients) in the Pediatric pool.
L2006: The most common AEs leading to dose modification of selumetinib were all consistent with the known safety profile of selumetinib or commonly noted in any pediatric population (ie, influenza-like illness).
L2007: In SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, the most commonly reported AEs leading to dose modification (interruption and/or reduction) of selumetinib were vomiting (12 [24.0%]), influenza-like illness and paronychia (7 [14.0%], each), diarrhoea (6 [12.0%]), and nausea (5 [10.0%]). All other AEs leading to modification of the selumetinib were reported in \le 4 (8.0%) patients. Similar observations and incidences of AEs were observed in the Pediatric pool. Overall, the incidence of AEs leading to dose modifications was higher in the Pediatric pool compared to the Adult monotherapy pool (78.4% compared with 29.4%, respectively), however the higher incidence of AEs leading to dose modifications in the pediatric population is most likely due to the longer duration of exposure in SPRINT compared to the adult monotherapy studies.
L2008: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2009: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's analysis of AEs leading to dose modifications (interruptions or reductions) of selumetinib in SPRINT Phase 2 Stratum 1 and across the pediatric pool as presented in Table 50 and across the adult safety pool as presented in Table 51.
L2010: Dose modifications for AEs occurred in nearly 80% of pediatric patients. The most common AEs leading to dose modifications in more than 10% of pediatric patients were vomiting, diarrhea, nausea, flu-like illnesses and paronychia. The majority of patients were able to resume dosing upon resolution of the AE without dose reduction.
L2011: Approximately 1/3 of patients in the pediatric pool required dose reduction due to an AE. AEs that required dose reduction in at least 2 patients were CPK elevation, increased weight, paronychia, stomatitis and rash. One patient required dose reduction for decreased ejection fraction.
L2012: Nearly 25% of patients in the adult pool receiving the hyd-sulfate formulation required dose modifications for AEs. Similar to the pediatric pool, gastrointestinal and skin toxicities most commonly led to dose interruptions. Five percent of patients in the adult hyd-sulfate cohort required dose reduction for AEs.
L2013: The FDA agrees that the increased incidence of dose interruptions and dose reductions in the pediatric pool as compared to the adult safety pool is due to the longer exposure time in pediatric patients. The FDA also acknowledges that some selumetinib interruptions in the pediatric population were due to common childhood illnesses that may not have been related to selumetinib exposure. Further, withholding drug for more mild-moderate toxicities may be more reasonable in a benign tumor population.
L2014: ## **Treatment Emergent Adverse Events and Adverse Reactions**
L2015: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2016: The safety databases for SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, the pediatric pool, and the adult monotherapy pool were analyzed at each level of the MedDRA hierarchy for treatment emergent AEs (TEAEs). The tables in this section summarize the incidence of TEAEs, defined as AEs that occurred from the time of the first dose up to 30 days after the last dose of selumetinib or data cutoff date if that date was earlier. Almost all pediatric patients (99%) and adult patients (98%) exposed to selumetinib had at least one AE during treatment.
L2017: The SPRINT data indicate that AEs were frequent and varied in the pediatric NF1 PN population. Table 52 represents the most frequently affected systems (&gt; 20% incidence) at the system organ class (SOC) level. Note that during SPRINT, laboratory values outside of normal range were reported as AEs regardless of clinical significance; therefore, nearly all patients experienced an AE falling under the Investigations category.

L2040: The FDA's analysis of the adult safety data demonstrated that AEs were less frequent and less varied in adult patients with cancer receiving selumetinib as compared to pediatric patients with NF1 PN. As previously discussed, this apparent difference is mostly due to the relatively short selumetinib exposure time for adults (median of 1.6 months for hyd-sulfate cohort) as compared to pediatric patients (median of 25.6 months for pediatric pool) in addition to inherent differences in the study populations.
L2041: Table 54 provides a side by side presentation of common AEs (\ge 20\%) occurring in the pediatric pool and the adult hyd-sulfate pool. Almost all AEs occurred more frequently in the pediatric pool. It is expected that many AEs (e.g., vomiting, diarrhea, abdominal pain, fever, headache, URI, nasal congestion, paronychia, stomatitis, sore throat, dermatitis, ear infection, gastroenteritis, pharyngitis) would be more prevalent in a pediatric study population.
