L2057: ## **Laboratory Findings**
L2058: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2059: Laboratory tests to assess hematology parameters, liver function (alkaline phosphatase, ALT, AST, bilirubin), chemistries (sodium, potassium, chloride, BUN, creatinine, glucose), minerals (magnesium, phosphorus, calcium), amylase, lipase, and CPK were measured at baseline and prior to each 28 day cycle for the first 5 cycles and then every other cycle thereafter during SPRINT. Table summarizes the common laboratory abnormalities for patients with NF1 PN receiving selumetinib during SPRINT. Incidence rates may differ from the laboratory abnormalities observed in the AE dataset because the denominator used to calculate the rate is based on the number of patients with a baseline value and at least one post-treatment value for that parameter rather than the safety population. Grade 3 or 4 chemistry abnormalities were uncommon; those that occurred in 2 or more of patients included anemia, neutropenia, hyperkalemia and increases in ALT, CPK and lipase.
L2060: Liver enzyme elevation was common but usually low grade during SPRINT. No patients in the pediatric pool had elevations of either ALT or AST preceding or concurrent with elevations in total bilirubin. In the adult supportive data, 7 patients with metastatic uveal melanoma in Study D1344C00001 (N=129; selumetinib or placebo in combination with dacarbazine) had concurrent elevation of liver enzymes and bilirubin. All patients had liver metastases and concurrent elevations in alkaline phosphatase; therefore, these events did not meet Hy's Law criteria.
L2061: CPK elevation was common during SPRINT. CPK elevation was not common in the adult data because CPK was not routinely assessed in early studies of selumetinib. CPK elevation and rhabdomyolysis are safety signals associated with the MEK inhibitor drug class. See Section 8.2.5.4 for a more detailed discussion of CPK elevation.

L2072: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2073: The Applicant's general statement that there were no unexpected changes in vital signs, ophthalmic or physical examination assessments in pediatric and adult patients treated with selumetinib is vague and potentially misleading. Ophthalmic assessments are discussed in Section 8.2.5.5.
L2074: Vital signs were collected once per cycle for the first 5 cycles and as clinically indicated during SPRINT. The CSRs for SPRINT indicate that vital sign changes were generally not a safety concern. Among the 74 patients in the pediatric pool, 16% (12/74) experience Grade 1-2 hypertension during selumetinib exposure. Hypertension AEs were not serious and did not
L2075: result in dose modification. Tachycardia or bradycardia occurred in 30% of patients and all AEs were Grade 1 except for two Grade 2 tachycardia AEs.

L2097: ## 8.2.5. Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
L2098: ## 8.2.5.1 Paronychia
L2099: ## Data:
L2100: See the Applicant's Position below.
L2101: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2102: Nail disorders have been identified as an adverse event of special interest (AESI) with selumetinib treatment. In general, reported events of nail disorders were non-serious, mild or moderate in severity, did not lead to treatment discontinuation, and generally resolved during continued treatment with selumetinib.
L2103: Within the grouped term of nail disorder events, all 23 AEs reported in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 were AEs of paronychia. AEs of paronychia were predominantly CTCAE Grade 1 or Grade 2; 3 (6.0%) patients had AEs of CTCAE Grade 3. The median time to onset was 306.0 days and the median duration was 96.0 days. Seven patients had a selumetinib dose interruption, 3 patients had a selumetinib dose reduction, and 1 patient experienced an AE of paronychia which led to permanent discontinuation of selumetinib. In SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, all of the AEs of paronychia were reported as resolved at the DCO.
L2104: The majority of the pediatric patients responded to dose modification and additional symptomatic/supportive treatment. At the time of DCO, ongoing events were either mild or moderate in severity. This observation can be attributed to the fact that inhibition of the mitogen activated protein kinase pathway results in growth arrest and premature differentiation of keratinocytes (Roberts and Der 2007). In addition, pediatric patients are more prone to acute paronychia through direct inoculation of fingers with flora from the mouth secondary to finger sucking and nail biting (Rockwell 2001).
L2105: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2106: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's analysis of paronychia in SPRINT Phase II, Stratum 1.
L2107: Paronychia is a toxicity that has been associated with a number of targeted agents including EGFR inhibitors and MEK inhibitors. The underlying mechanism of skin and nail toxicities associated with use of these agents may be via modifications of signals through the RAS/RAF/MEK/ ERK pathway, affecting proliferation and differentiation of epidermis cells.
L2108: Paronychia (FDA grouped PTs: paronychia and nail infection) was a common AE and occurred in 47% (35/74) of patients in the pediatric pool including 10% (7/74) of patients who experienced Grade 3 paronychia. Four patients required dose reductions for paronychia including one patient who was dose-reduced twice prior to permanently discontinuing selumetinib for paronychia. Except for 11 patients with unknown outcomes, the rest of the patients with paronychia recovered with symptomatic treatment and supportive care.
L2109: Whereas most other common AEs in the pediatric pool occurred at increased incidence rates in the first year of exposure and decreased in frequency and severity thereafter, paronychia occurred at a higher incidence after one year of selumetinib exposure and a higher incidence of CTCAE Grade \ge 2 events were observed in the second year of treatment.
L2110: The adult hyd-sulfate safety pool demonstrated less frequency and severity of paronychia with a 1% (1/79) incidence rate and no Grade 3-4 events. In addition to pediatric patients being more prone to paronychia than adults, the much higher incidence rate in the pediatric pool is reflective of the longer pediatric exposure time and frequency of late onset paronychia events.
L2111: ## 8.2.5.2 Alopecia [Reported Term of Hair Thinning] and Hair Colour Changes
L2112: ## Data:
L2113: See the Applicant's Position below.
L2114: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2115: Based on the data in pediatric and in adult patients, this medical concept of hair changes (includes the PT of alopecia and hair colour changes) was considered as an ADR for selumetinib.
L2116: In the Pediatric pool, 22 (29.7%) patients reported at least 1 AE of alopecia and 20 (27%) patients reported at least 1 AE of hair colour changes; 13 of 74 patients (17.5%) experienced both alopecia (reported term: hair thinning) and hair colour changes (reported term: hair lightening or lightening of hair color). All the reported AEs were Grade 1 and did not require dose interruption or reduction. A higher number of hair change cases were noted in Pediatric pool compared with the known safety data in adult cancer patients. In the Adult monotherapy pool a total of 21 (6.1%) patients reported AEs in the medical concept of Hair changes (PT alopecia [reported term: hair thinning] 16 [4.6%] patients, PT hair colour changes 7 [2.0%] patients). While generally hair changes are noted in pediatric populations, it is unclear why hair changes may be more prominent or more frequently observed in pediatric patients on selumetinib than in adult patients.
L2117: Schad et al 2010 have observed reduced pigmentation of hair in patients with unresectable melanoma Stage III/IV treated with 100 mg BID dose of selumetinib. This was correlated with selumetinib treatment, with repigmentation occurring upon treatment discontinuation. Similar
L2118: findings have been seen in patients treated with c-Kit-inhibiting agents (Moss et al 2003). Inhibition of c-Kit leads to reduced mitogen-activated protein kinase activity and a diminished expression of its downstream transcription factor microphthalmia-associated transcription factor (regulating the expression of tyrosinase mRNA), which regulates both melanocyte proliferation and melanogenesis.
L2119: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2120: The FDA grouped alopecia, hair color change, and hair disorder PTs for the analysis. Using this grouping, 39% (29/74) of patients in the pediatric pool and 6% (5/79) of patients in the adult hyd-sulfate pool experienced a hair disorder adverse reaction during selumetinib exposure. There were no Grade 3 or higher hair disorder AEs in either population. The FDA did not review the referenced literature regarding potential drug mechanism for this ADR.
L2121: ## **8.2.5.3 Ejection Fraction Decreased**
L2122: ## **Data:**
L2123: See the Applicant's Position below.
L2124: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2125: Clinical programs for MEK inhibitors have all included monitoring of left ventricular function with multigated acquisition or echocardiography (patients with a history of clinically significant cardiovascular disease were excluded from participating in clinical trials). Asymptomatic or symptomatic LVEF decrease/left ventricular dysfunction is generally fully reversible with discontinuation of MEK inhibition or with dose reduction when the dysfunction is not severe or symptomatic (Flaherty et al 2012a; Flaherty et al 2012b; Abdel-Rahman et al 2015; Banks et al 2017).
L2126: In SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, AEs in the sub-group term of ejection fraction decreased events were reported for 22.0% (11 of 50) patients which were all CTCAE Grade 2, no events of a higher CTCAE Grade severity reported. The median time to first onset of AEs in the sub group term of ejection fraction decreased events was 226.0 days, with a median duration of 77.5 days. None of the events resulted in dose modification or discontinuation, and where event outcome was known (8 patients), all patients recovered.
L2127: All cases of ejection fraction decreased in the Pediatric pool were identified during routine investigations. These were not accompanied by symptoms suggestive of cardiac failure/impairment, such as edema, dyspnea, palpitations, fatigue, or syncope. The majority of AEs of ejection fraction decreased were a numerical reduction which met the criteria value as a decrease from baseline but remained in the normal range.
L2128: As pediatric patients with a history of impaired left ventricular function or a baseline LVEF below institutional LLN have not been studied, labelling guidance suggests LVEF should be
L2129: evaluated before initiation of treatment to establish baseline values and ensure that they are above the LLN. Reduction in LVEF can be managed using treatment interruption, dose reduction or treatment discontinuation.
L2130: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2131: The FDA agrees with the summary data provided by the Applicant for EF decreases observed in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and notes that the Applicant submitted corrected data for resolution rates for EF decrease events subsequent to the analysis described above. Of the 11 patients with EF decreases, 6 recovered and 5 had unknown outcomes.
L2132: Across the pediatric pool, 17% (23/74) of patients experienced EF decrease including one Grade 3 LVEF decrease (EF decrease from 65% to 50%) resulting in dose reduction. All EF decrease events were asymptomatic and detected on routine echocardiograms performed during the study. During SPRINT, a numerical drop of 10% or more from baseline was counted as an EF decrease AE. Fourteen of the 17 patients who experienced EF decrease maintained EFs that met the study entry criterion of EF \ge 53\%. The other 3 patients had an EF decrease to 50% (n=2) or 52% (n=1).
L2133: In adult selumetinib studies, EF decrease events were generally considered AEs if the EF decreased below the institutional lower limit of normal. In the adult hyd-sulfate pool, 3% (2/79) of patients experienced left ventricular dysfunction AEs. Both events were Grade 3 and resulted in permanent discontinuation of selumetinib. Both patients recovered. Across the larger adult monotherapy pool, there were two grade 5 cardiac events: congestive heart failure and cardio-respiratory arrest. These events are described in Section 8.2.4 under Deaths.
L2134: Ventricular dysfunction, including EF decrease, is a known MEK inhibitor-related toxicity. During SPRINT, patients underwent baseline echocardiogram and then routine echocardiograms every 4 cycles for the first year of treatment and every 6 cycles thereafter. There were specific management guidelines for providers based on the level of LVEF decrease and whether the patient was symptomatic. Pediatric cardiology consultation was the usual course of action. Sections 2 and 5 of the product label will provide similar guidelines for prescribers.
L2135: ## 8.2.5.4 Blood CPK Increase
L2136: ## **Data:**
L2137: See the Applicant's Position below.
L2138: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2139: Effects on muscle function have been identified as an AESI with selumetinib treatment. In general, muscle function events were mild or moderate in severity, rarely led to treatment
L2140: discontinuation, were manageable by standard therapies, and generally resolved during continued treatment with selumetinib.
L2141: In the Pediatric pool, AEs in the sub-group term of CPK increased events were reported by most patients (87.8%; 65 of 74 patients). AEs were predominantly CTCAE Grade 1 or Grade 2 (74.3% patients); with few patients reporting AEs of CTCAE Grade 3 (6.8%) or CTCAE Grade 4 (4.1%). The median time to first onset of AEs in the sub-group term of CPK increased events was 84.0 days, with a median duration of 143.0 days.
L2142: In SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1, 38 (76.0%) patients experienced an AE of blood CPK increased. For the maximum CTCAE grade events that patients experienced, the median time to onset was 105.5 days and the median duration was 126.0 days. The majority of events were Grade 1 or 2 and resolved with no change in selumetinib dose. CTCAE Grade \ge 3 events occurred in 3 (6%) patients and 1 (2.0%) patient (Patient [b] (6)) had an AE of CTCAE Grade 4. Only 1 (2.0%) patient (Patient [b] (6)) in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 had an AE which led to selumetinib dose interruption followed by dose reduction; none of the AEs required dose discontinuation.
L2143: CPK increased is not uncommon in children. CPK levels are known to transiently rise after physical activity. The degree of CPK elevation depends on the type and duration of physical activity, with greater elevation in those who do not do such activity consistently (Moghadam-Kia et al 2016). There are also reports which suggests that there is a small reduction in CPK levels as people age (Lilleng et al 2011). There was no evidence of muscular AEs such as myalgia or muscular weakness in association with selumetinib-induced CPK increases, with the exception of a small number of reports where other more plausible aetiologies such as vertebral disc prolapse and muscle exertion were identified.
L2144: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2145: The FDA agrees with Applicant's analysis of CPK events across the pediatric pool. The PTs included in the 88% event rate were CPK elevation, musculoskeletal pain, muscular weakness and myalgia. The FDA does not fully agree with the Applicant's discussion of CPK elevation in children as compared to adults. While the FDA acknowledges that CPK levels may be higher after physical activity, the Moghadam-Kia reference also notes that levels normalize in most patients within seven days of onset which was not typical when CPK elevation duration was assessed in the pediatric pool.
L2146: Rhabdomyolysis is a rare and serious adverse reaction associated with the MEK inhibitor class. The FDA reviewed pediatric and adult safety data for rhabdomyolysis, CPK elevation, and CPK elevation that was concurrent with muscle symptoms and/or renal dysfunction. Given the relatively small pediatric safety pool in the original NDA and the short median exposure time for the 79 adult patients who received the hyd-sulfate formulation, the FDA requested that the Applicant submit additional analyses of CPK elevation and rhabdomyolysis using the global adult safety database, selumetinib expanded access program in PN, and externally-sponsored
L2147: selumetinib studies. The following analyses of rhabdomyolysis and CPK elevation consider the review of these data sources in addition to the pediatric and adult monotherapy datasets in the original NDA.
L2148: ## *Rhabdomyolysis*
L2149: There were no events of rhabdomyolysis in the pediatric pool or in pediatric patients treated in externally-sponsored studies, or in the selumetinib PN expanded access program. The Applicant identified two cases of rhabdomyolysis in adult patients across 36 externally-sponsored trials. Both patients received selumetinib during Study D1532C00049, an NCI-sponsored study of selumetinib monotherapy in adult patients with relapsed/refractory multiple myeloma.
L2150: *Patient* [REDACTED](b) (6): 69 yo with myeloma and medical history significant for prior chemotherapy, stroke, prostate cancer, and rheumatoid heat disease, presented after three weeks of selumetinib exposure with chest pain and Grade 4 thrombocytopenia. He developed acute renal failure (creatinine of 5.4 mg/dL) three days later. He was diagnosed with rhabdomyolysis 5 days later. No CPK values were provided. The patient was hospitalized, had a negative sepsis workup, was started on dialysis, and received plasmapheresis for 2 days. His creatinine continued to rise, and he went into respiratory failure, was transferred to hospice care and died. The events of rhabdomyolysis, acute kidney injury, and decreased platelet count were considered related to both selumetinib and to progression of primary disease by the investigator.
L2151: *Patient* [REDACTED](b) (6): 77 yo with multiple myeloma and medical history significant for prior chemotherapy, immunotherapy and hematopoietic stem cell transplantation, presented to clinic after a 2 month exposure to selumetinib and laboratory assessments showed elevated BUN and creatinine, myoglobin increase (308 ng/mL) and CPK increase (427 IU/L). The patient was hospitalized for Grade 3 rhabdomyolysis and acute kidney injury. Selumetinib was discontinued. The patient was treated with intravenous fluids, and both CPK and myoglobin levels were reported to have decreased. The patient improved and was discharged after 3 days. The investigator attributed the kidney injury and rhabdomyolysis to selumetinib.
L2152: **Reviewer:** While both of these cases are confounded by the patients' underlying disease, the reviewer agrees with the investigators' assessments. Given the known safety profile of the MEK inhibitor class, selumetinib should be considered at least possibly related to these rhabdomyolysis events.
L2153: ## *CPK elevation*
L2154: Across the pediatric pool, CPK elevation occurred in 76% (56/74) of patients including 10% (7/74) of patients with \ge Grade 3 CPK increases and 3% (2/74) of patients with Grade 4 CPK elevation. Two patients had SAEs of CPK elevation, and these cases are discussed in Section 8.4.2 under SAEs. There were no permanent discontinuations for CPK elevation. Seven percent
L2155: (5/74) of patients required dose reductions for Grade 3 or 4 CPK elevation. The median time to onset of CPK elevation was 106 days and the median duration of CPK elevation was 95 days.
L2156: Eleven patients (15%) in the pediatric pool experienced concurrent (\ge one day overlap) CPK and creatinine increases at least once including 3 patients from SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 who experienced concurrent or subsequent (within 30 days) muscle symptoms. Of these, 1 patient (b) (6), reported musculoskeletal pain concurrent with blood CPK increase and creatinine increase and is described below. In the other 2 patients, the musculoskeletal pain occurred concurrently with Grade 1 creatinine elevation, but there was no overlap with increased CPK.
L2157: *Patient* (b) (6): Grade 2 musculoskeletal pain occurred concurrently with Grade 1 CPK elevation and Grade 2 creatinine increased. The overlap between musculoskeletal pain and blood CPK increased was 1 day; the overlap between musculoskeletal pain and blood creatinine increased was for 29 days. The investigator assessed the musculoskeletal pain as not related to selumetinib and the CPK increase as related. The events resolved without dose modification.
L2158: Five patients (7%) experienced myalgia or muscle weakness concurrent with CPK elevation. None of these patients showed evidence of renal dysfunction.
L2159: *Patient* (b) (6): Grade 1 myalgia concurrent with Grade 1 blood CPK increased for 6 days. Both resolved without dose modification and were attributed by the investigator to patient carrying a heavy back pack and to selumetinib.
L2160: *Patient* (b) (6): Two Grade 1 myalgia events overlapped with ongoing Grade 1 CPK elevation. The investigator considered both events of myalgia as unrelated to selumetinib.
L2161: *Patient* (b) (6): This patient experienced multiple events of myalgia, muscular weakness and CPK elevation during the study. There was one Grade 3 event of muscle weakness that was concurrent with Grade 1 CPK elevation that resolved. There was one isolated event of Grade 3 CPK elevation resulting in dose reduction. The investigator attributed CPK elevation to selumetinib; the muscle weakness was attributed to a disc prolapse.
L2162: *Patient* (b) (6): Three AEs of Grade 1 myalgia including one event that overlapped with an ongoing Grade 1 CPK elevation. Both events resolved without dose modification. The investigator attributed both events to selumetinib treatment.
L2163: *Patient* (b) (6): This case is summarized under Section 8.2.4 Discontinuation AEs.
L2164: **Reviewer:** CPK elevation was common in pediatric patients exposed to selumetinib; however, there were no rhabdomyolysis events reported in the pediatric population. Patients who experienced concurrent CPK elevation and creatinine elevation and/or muscle symptoms
L2165: *generally had Grade 1-2 events that resolved without dose reduction. CPK elevation rates were not reliable in the adult monotherapy pool because not all studies required routine assessments. There were rare cases of rhabdomyolysis in the adult global data. Given the risks for CPK elevation and rhabdomyolysis with MEK inhibitors, the FDA included CPK elevation as a warning in Section 5 of the product label with specific dose modification guidelines in Section 2.*
L2166: ## 8.2.5.5 Blurred Vision (Visual Disturbances)
L2167: ## **Data:**
L2168: See the Applicant's Position below.
L2169: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2170: Ocular toxicity including vision blurred, csr, retinal pigment epithelial detachment (RPED) and RVO is a known ADR for selumetinib monotherapy and all adult and pediatric protocols contained specific toxicity management guidelines in place for management of these events. A detailed analysis of AEs within retinal events (consisting of the standardised MedDRA query of retinal disorders, RPED events, csr events, RVO events, other retinal disorders) was performed. Retinal events that were reported by 2 or more patients in the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 data included vision blurred (4 of 50 patients, 8.0%) and photophobia (2 of 50 patients, 4.0%), which were reported at a similar frequency in the Pediatric pool.
L2171: Effects on the eye have been identified as an AESI with selumetinib treatment. In general, retinal effects were mild or moderate in severity, did not lead to treatment discontinuation, were manageable by standard supportive therapies, and generally resolved during continued treatment with selumetinib.
L2172: Blurred vision has been reported in both pediatric and adult populations; events were generally CTCAE Grade 1. AEs consistent with csr/RPED and with RVO have also been reported in a small number of patients receiving treatment with selumetinib, generally in combination with other anti-cancer agents targeting AKT inhibition or IGFR-IR inhibition, or with cytotoxic chemotherapies. Blurred vision was reported by 4 patients (8.0%) in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 and at a similar frequency in the Pediatric pool (9.5%). The median time to onset of blurred vision in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 was 455.5 days, and the event rate (per 100 patient years) was 4.03.
L2173: AEs in the sub-group terms of RVO events, csr events, and RPED events were not reported for any patients in the Pediatric pool.
L2174: Within the subgroup term of RVO events, csr events, and RPED events, no events were reported for any patients in the Pediatric pool. This was consistent with the observation that no retinal involvement was observed in the ophthalmic examinations of pediatric patients. In
L2175: adults an isolated case of RVO was reported (after double-blind treatment with selumetinib + dacarbazine in the Study D1344C00001).
L2176: (b) (6)
L2177: Guidance in the proposed label advises that,
L2178: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2179: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's incidence rates for retinal and visual AEs in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1.
L2180: Ocular toxicity is an adverse reaction associated with the MEK inhibitor class. RVO is rare, but can result in blindness. RPED, a retinal detachment at the retinal pigment epithelium layer, includes central serous retinopathy (CSR), choriorretinopathy, and detachment of the retinal pigment epithelium events. Other more common ocular AEs associated with MEK inhibition include blurred vision, periorbital edema and dry eyes.
L2181: The FDA reviewed the pediatric and adult safety pools data for retinal AEs, visual impairment, and other ocular AEs. Given the rarity of some serious ocular AEs (RVO, RPED) and the relatively small pediatric safety pool, and the short median exposure time for the adult data pool, the FDA requested that the Applicant provide additional analyses of ocular toxicity across the global adult safety database, selumetinib expanded access program, and externally-sponsored selumetinib studies. The following analyses consider the review of these data sources in addition to the pediatric and adult monotherapy data pools in the original NDA.
L2182: Across the pediatric pool, 33% (24/74) of patients experienced at least one eye disorder AE (PTs included in Eye disorders SOC). There were no SAEs, AEs \ge Grade 3, discontinuation AEs or dose reductions for ocular toxicity in the pediatric pool. In the adult hyd-sulfate pool, 27% of patients experienced an eye disorder during selumetinib including one Grade 3 visual impairment and one Grade 2 RVO resulting in discontinuation of selumetinib. Five percent (4/79) of adult patients experienced RPED or choriorretinopathy. These events were not serious or severe and did not result in dose modification. See Table 56.

L2191: The FDA reviewed the selumetinib expanded access program data and the externally-sponsored pediatric studies data for additional pediatric ocular toxicities. In the PN expanded access program, 3 pediatric patients had ocular SAEs (visual field defect, eye disorder, abnormal ocular finding). The patient with the visual field defect discontinued selumetinib. In the pediatric external study dataset, 2 patients experienced optic nerve disorders resulting in discontinuation, and one patient experienced RPED (see summary narrative below). The FDA notes that this dataset is confounded by patients having underlying low grade glioma, and in some cases, existence of baseline visual impairments due to underlying disease.
L2192: **RPED SAE:** 13 yo with pilocytic astrocytoma affecting the optic nerve presented during Cycle 6 with vision impairment. According to the narrative, gross examination of the eyes was
L2193: unremarkable. An undilated fundal examination revealed mild hyperlucent pigmentary changes in the central macula bilaterally. The visual fields were full in the left eye and diminished in the infratemporal portion of the right eye. On optical coherence tomography (OCT) the macula revealed the beginning of the separation of the retinal pigmented epithelium in both eyes at the fovea. Selumetinib was discontinued. The patient recovered. The ophthalmologist considered the retinal detachment causally-related to selumetinib.
L2194: The Applicant's search of the adult global database of 1860 patients who received selumetinib as single agent or in combination with other agents revealed 0.2% (4/1860) patients with RVO and 0.1% (1/1860) RPED events. The four patients who experienced RVO events had Grade 2 (n=3) or Grade 1 (n=1) events. These patients were treated with combination regimens. Two of the 4 patients are reported to not have recovered. Across the externally-sponsored adult studies, there was an additional RVO event and 6 RPED events with limited information regarding outcomes.
L2195: **Reviewer:** Selumetinib appears to have a similar ocular risk profile as other commercially available MEK inhibitors. While serious toxicities such as RVO and RPED occurred rarely in the pediatric data, there is no evidence that pediatric patients have less risk than adult patients for MEK-associated retinal AEs. An FDA ophthalmology consult was obtained to make recommendations on mitigating risk for serious ocular toxicity. These recommendations are included in Sections 2 and 5 of the product label.
L2196: ## 8.2.5.6 Rash
L2197: ## **Data:**
L2198: See the Applicant's Position below.
L2199: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2200: Acneiform rash and other (non-acneiform) rashes have been identified as AESIs with selumetinib treatment. The pediatric study protocols had specific toxicity management guidelines in place for management of these events.
L2201: In the Pediatric pool, the AEs in the grouped term of acneiform rash were reported for 54.1% (40 of 74) patients, all of which were the term of dermatitis acneiform. AEs were predominantly CTCAE Grade 1 or Grade 2 (38 [51.4%] patients) with few (2 [2.7%]) patients reporting CTCAE Grade 3 dermatitis acneiform. The median time to first onset of AEs in the grouped term of acneiform rash was 27.0 days, with a median duration of 111.0 days. Observation regarding the incidence, severity, time to onset and duration of acneiform rash were similar in the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1. Generally, it was observed that acneiform rash was observed in postpubertal patients (&gt;12 years). Dermatitis acneiform occurred at a higher
L2202: frequency in patients aged 12 to 16 years of age (95.8%, 23 of 24 patients) compared to patients aged \ge 2 to 11 years (26.7%, 12 of 45 patients).
L2203: In the Pediatric pool, AEs in the grouped term of non-acneiform rash were reported for 64.9% (48 of 74 patients, patients predominantly pruritus (41.9%) and rash maculopapular (39.2%). AEs were predominantly CTCAE Grade 1 or Grade 2 (46 [62.2%] patients); 2 (2.7%) patients had AEs of CTCAE Grade 3. The median time to first onset of AEs in the grouped term of non acneiform rash was 190.5 days, with a median duration of 34.0 days. Observation regarding the incidence, severity, time to onset and duration of acneiform rash were similar in the SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1.
L2204: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2205: Skin toxicity is part of the safety profile of MEK inhibitors. The FDA's analysis of rash was based on data from the pediatric pool and the adult hyd-sulfate pool. The search strategy for relevant PTs included use of the following HLTs: acnes, bullous conditions, dermatitis and eczema, erythemas, exfoliative conditions, rashes, eruptions and exanthems, and urticarias. Rashes were very common during SPRINT. The majority of rashes were Grade 1-2 and did not require dose modification. There were no SAEs and no discontinuations due to rash. Three patients required dose reduction for Grade 3 maculopapular rash (n=2) and Grade 3 urticaria (n=1).
L2206: Dermatitis acneiform occurred in more than half of the patients, and as the Applicant notes, was more prevalent in the adolescent population. In the age subgroup 2-11 years, 27% (12/45) of patients experienced dermatitis acneiform. In patients older than 12 years, 97% (28/29) developed acneiform rash during selumetinib exposure. Table 57 summarizes the various rashes that occurred in the pediatric pool.

L2213: There were no events of palmar-plantar erythrodysesthesia syndrome (PPES) in the pediatric pool. Pruritis occurred in 43% (32/74) of patients in the pediatric pool. Pruritis events were nonserious and Grade 1-2 in severity. One patient required dose reduction for pruritis.
L2214: Among the adult patients who received the hyd-sulfate capsule, 73% (58/79) experienced at least one rash AE during selumetinib exposure including 8% (6/79) Grade 3 rashes. Similar to the pediatric pool, dermatitis acneiform was common and occurred in 35% (28/79) of patients including one Grade 3 rash that resulted in permanent discontinuation of selumetinib. PPES occurred in 6% (5/79) of patients including one Grade 3 event requiring interruption of selumetinib. Pruritis occurred in 14% (11/79) of patients, and all events were Grade 1-2.
L2215: ## *Skin Rash and Associated Infections*
L2216: Across the pediatric pool, concurrent rashes and skin infections were not common, and in the cases where these events occurred in a temporal relationship, causality was confounded (e.g. staphylococcal infections in patients with underlying chronic eczema, ringworm). The Applicant provided an analysis of concurrent skin rashes and infections across the adult monotherapy pool of 347 patients. Six percent (21/347) had concurrent rash and skin infection or skin infection events within 30 days of rash. All infections were Grade 1-2 except for one patient with an SAE of Grade 3 cellulitis
L2217: *Patient* (b) (6): 58 yo male developed diffuse rash on Day 11 which was present for 50 days. Approximately 4 weeks after the rash started, the patient experienced Grade 3 right leg cellulitis. The patient presents with fever, cellulitis and associated lymphedema of the right leg. Selumetinib was interrupted. While the patient was in the hospital, imaging revealed progressive disease, and the patient did not restart selumetinib. The patient recovered from right leg cellulitis.
L2218: **Reviewer:** *Skin toxicities including dermatitis acneiform, nonacneiform rashes, pruritis and dry skin were common during selumetinib treatment. The majority of events were Grade 1-2 in severity and did not require dose modifications in pediatric patients. Despite the frequency of rashes, it did not appear that rashes led to serious skin infections in patients with NF1 PN. PPES did not occur in the pediatric population; however, this is a known risk with MEK inhibitors and other targeted agents. This toxicity was observed in the adult selumetinib data. Given the frequency of skin toxicity associated with selumetinib, the product label includes a warning in Section 5 and specific dose modification guidelines in Section 2.*
L2219: ## 8.2.5.6 Physeal Dysplasia
L2220: ## Data:
L2221: See the Applicant's Position below.
L2222: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2223: Physeal dysplasia has been identified as a potential risk for selumetinib treatment, based on data from nonclinical studies. No AEs of physeal dysplasia were reported for the Pediatric pool or for the Adult monotherapy pool. In order to ascertain the risk of physeal dysplasia, height was used as a surrogate to ascertain the effect of selumetinib on growth plates.
L2224: Data of the growth centiles from the Pediatric pool were overlaid with the growth centiles from patients on the Natural history study, which acted as a control population. Based on this data, selumetinib does not appear to have any adverse effects on skeletal growth in patients enrolled in SPRINT. This data is also supportive of an independent data analysis done for the SPRINT Phase I data by NCI/CTEP.
L2225: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2226: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment and notes that growth and development parameters will continue to be monitored as part of the long-term safety follow-up for SPRINT and as part of the post-marketing requirements for selumetinib.
L2227: ## 8.2.5.7 Serious Gastrointestinal Toxicity
L2228: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2229: GI toxicity is part of the safety profile of the MEK inhibitor class. Serious GI toxicity is a labeled warning in the trametinib package insert based on observed events of colitis and fatal GI perforation. Colitis is labeled as an important adverse reaction in the binimetinib package insert. Given the class safety signal and the observation of frequent GI toxicity during SPRINT, the FDA considers GI toxicity to be an additional AESI for selumetinib.
L2230: Across the pediatric pool, GI toxicity was common. The FDA acknowledges the relatively long median exposure time to selumetinib and that diarrhea, vomiting and abdominal pain are more common complaints in a pediatric population as compared to adults. FDA therefore considered severity of the events, impact on dosing, the investigators' assessments of relatedness, and events per patient in the overall risk assessment.
L2231: Diarrhea occurred in 77% of patients including 15% with Grade 3 events. One patient required dose reduction followed by permanent discontinuation for diarrhea. Investigators assessed diarrhea as possibly or probably related to selumetinib in 65% (48/74) of patients. The median number of diarrhea AEs per patients was 4 (range: 1-29). Per protocol, patients on SPRINT started loperamide or other anti-diarrheal agents at the onset of the first loose stool.
L2232: Vomiting occurred in 82% (61/74) of patients including 8% with Grade 3 events. Vomiting AEs did not result in discontinuation or dose reductions, but 25% of patients required drug interruptions for vomiting. Investigators assessed vomiting as possibly or probably related to selumetinib in 72% (53/74) of patients. The median number of vomiting AEs per patients was 4 (range: 1-20).
L2233: Stomatitis was also common in the pediatric pool. Fifty percent of patients experienced at least one occurrence of stomatitis; however, there were no \ge Grade 3 events.
L2234: Serious GI toxicity in the pediatric pool was uncommon. There were no intestinal obstruction or perforation events during SPRINT. One patient had two episodes of colitis that were considered unrelated to selumetinib and possibly related to prior antibiotic use. One of the events was Grade 3 and required hospitalization for intravenous fluids, metronidazole and vancomycin.
L2235: Given the rarity of some serious GI toxicities, the relatively small pediatric safety pool, and the short median exposure time for the adult monotherapy pool, the FDA requested that the
L2236: Applicant provide additional analyses of serious GI toxicity including colitis, ileus, intestinal obstruction and intestinal perforation, across the global adult safety database, selumetinib PN expanded access program, and externally-sponsored selumetinib studies.
L2237: ## *Serious GI Toxicity: Global Database*
L2238: The Applicant conducted an analysis of the safety database for AZ-sponsored trials of selumetinib as a single agent or in combination using preferred terms included under the GI Disorders SOC and SMQ for gastrointestinal perforation, ulceration, hemorrhage or obstruction. Using this search strategy, among 1449 patients, 8% (113/1449) experienced GI SAEs including 0.4% (6/1449) Grade 5 events. Four percent (55/1449) of patients experienced SAEs considered at least possibly related to selumetinib. The following causally related SAEs occurred in more than one patient: abdominal pain (N=5, 0.3%), colitis (N=3, 0.2%), and ileus (N=2, 0.1%). Four patients experienced fatal events deemed related to selumetinib: chemical peritonitis, abdominal pain, hematemesis, and upper GI hemorrhage. Except for one Grade 5 abdominal pain event ( [REDACTED] (b) (6) ), discussed in Section 8.2.4 under Deaths), all other Grade 4 and 5 SAEs occurred in patients receiving selumetinib as part of combination regimens.
L2239: ## Grade 5 Events
L2240: *Patient* [REDACTED] (b) (6) : Grade 5 abdominal pain. See Section 8.2.4 under Deaths
L2241: *Patient* [REDACTED] (b) (6) : Grade 5 Upper Gastrointestinal Hemorrhage. 68 yo male with metastatic Stage IV NSCLC with pleural effusion. Relevant concomitant medications included omeprazole, sodium picox sulfate, morphine, ondansetron, metoclopramide, and loperamide. The patient presented with Grade 3 diarrhea on Day 2. The patient had blood in his stool. He was hospitalized for IV fluids and electrolyte replacement. Hb was 8.6, decreased from enrollment Hb of 10.9. Endoscopy was performed and showed no sites of active bleeding and he was diagnosed with nonerosive gastritis. The diarrhea resolved. On Day 33, the patient experienced upper gastrointestinal hemorrhage, with profuse bleeding through the nose and mouth at home followed by hematemesis and death. The primary cause of death was upper digestive tract bleeding and the secondary cause of death was NSCLC. Autopsy was not performed. Investigator assessed the event as related to selumetinib and unrelated to docetaxel.
L2242: **Reviewer:** *Selumetinib cannot be excluded from causality analysis, but this case is confounded by the presence of baseline gastritis at enrollment.*
L2243: *Patient* [REDACTED] (b) (6) : *Grade 5 chemical peritonitis/gastrointestinal perforation.* 67 yo male with metastatic NSCLC. Medical history significant for completion of 6 cycles of cisplatin and pemetrexed one month prior to initiation of selumetinib and docetaxel. The patient was diagnosed with chemical peritonitis on Day 66. He received metronidazole, nefopam and paracetamol. An endoscopy was performed and biliary peritonitis was observed but no perforation was reported. The investigator assessed the peritonitis with pneumoperitoneum to be associated with gastrointestinal perforation leading to septic shock and acute renal failure.
L2244: The cause of death was initially reported as peritonitis; however, on follow-up report, the cause of death was changed to gastrointestinal perforation 'not proved'. Autopsy was not performed. The investigator assessed the fatal event as related to selumetinib and docetaxel.
L2245: **Reviewer:** *Selumetinib cannot be excluded in the causality analysis; however, there is limited information to make a reliable assessment in this case. Peritonitis and pneumoperitoneum could certainly lead to intestinal perforation and septic shock. It is not clear how the patient presented at the time of diagnosis with peritonitis or when the endoscopy was performed relative to the patient's death.*
L2246: **Patient** (b) (6): Grade 5 hematemesis. 84 yo male with metastatic NSCLC treated with prior carboplatin and paclitaxel. On Day 42, the patient had Cycle 3 lab assessment showing Hb 9 g/dL, INR 1.03, P 12.1 seconds and platelet count of 177. On Day 46, the patients had Grade 2 vomiting for 3 days which was reported as resolved. On Day 52, the patient experienced hematemesis and was taken to the emergency room. The hematemesis worsened and the patient was reported to have died on the same day. No endoscopy was performed. The causes of death were reported as lung cancer, multiple organ failure and hematemesis. Autopsy was not performed. The event hematemesis was assessed by the investigator as related to selumetinib and docetaxel.
L2247: **Reviewer:** *There is insufficient information to make a definitive causal assessment, but selumetinib cannot be excluded from the attribution analysis.*
L2248: ## Grade 4 SAEs
L2249: The Applicant's search also identified a Grade 4 gastric ulcer perforation event and a Grade 4 ileus event that were serious and considered causally related to selumetinib.
L2250: **Patient** (b) (6): *Grade 4 gastric ulcer perforation.* 60 yo male with metastatic NSCLC and medical history significant for gastritis, diabetes, HTN, and 8 prior cycles of etoposide and cisplatin. The patient experienced Grade 3 vomiting on Day 86 and docetaxel was discontinued. On Day 125, the patient was admitted with an acute abdomen and diagnosed intraoperatively with a Grade 4 gastric ulcer perforation. The patient was transferred to the ICU and was also being treated for a Grade 2 pneumonia. The patient improved and was discharged after 29 days in the hospital. The event of gastric ulcer perforation was assessed by the investigator as related to selumetinib but not docetaxel and resulted in discontinuation.
L2251: **Patient** (b) (6): Grade 4 ileus. 60 yo male with NSCLC with adrenal and brain metastases. Medical history significant for ileus and hernia repair and prior cisplatin and pemetrexed. The patient was taking metoclopramide for nausea and naloxone for pain. On Day 23, the patient was hospitalized for Grade 4 ileus and underwent surgery with ileal resection the next day. Immunohistochemistry analysis of the tissue showed findings which were consistent with metastasizing adenocarcinoma. Selumetinib was discontinued on Day 23. Four days after the
L2252: surgery, the patient developed sepsis and died. No autopsy was performed. The investigator assessed the ileus and the sepsis as related to both selumetinib and docetaxel.
L2253: **Reviewer:** There are confounders in both of these cases, particularly the finding of a developing metastatic lesion in the ileum in the latter patient; however, given that MEK inhibitors are known to be associated with serious GI toxicities including perforation, selumetinib cannot be excluded as a potential causal factor in either event.
L2254: ## ***Serious GI Toxicity: Expanded Access Program and Externally-Sponsored Trials***
L2255: There were no events of ileus, intestinal obstruction or intestinal perforation in the selumetinib PN expanded access program. There was one serious colitis event in the context of sepsis and UTI diagnosed the same day and considered unrelated to selumetinib by the investigator.
L2256: There were 11 events of colitis, ileus, intestinal obstruction or intestinal perforation events from externally-sponsored research studies including one fatal intestinal obstruction, one colitis in a pediatric patient, and one intestinal obstruction in an adult patient with PN. Limited information was available for these events. The fatal obstruction event was considered unrelated to selumetinib and related to progressive metastatic cervical cancer. The colitis event was Grade 3, assessed as infectious and unrelated to selumetinib, and the patient recovered. The patient with PN experienced nausea and Grade 3 small intestinal obstruction 9 days after starting selumetinib. The investigator considered the events unrelated to selumetinib
L2257: **Reviewer:** In summary, GI toxicity is MEK inhibitor class effect. GI toxicity during the SPRINT trial was frequent but usually tolerable and patients were managed per protocol with supportive care measures and interruptions. Serious GI toxicities including perforation, obstruction and hemorrhage events were not observed across the pediatric pool which had relatively long exposure to selumetinib. However, more serious GI toxicities were identified across the global selumetinib datasets. The incidence was rare, but selumetinib was considered causally related in the events summarized above. The FDA considers that the pediatric PN population under the labeled indication is at risk for developing serious GI toxicity associated with use of selumetinib and should be monitored carefully. The product label attempts to mitigate risk by including specific precautions and dose modification guidelines. Additionally, this risk will be continually assessed during the long-term follow up of pediatric patients with PN in the post marketing phase.
L2258: ## 8.2.5.8 Hemorrhage
L2259: Hemorrhage is a labeled warning in commercially available MEK inhibitors including trametinib, binimetinib and cobimetinib. The safety data demonstrating this risk was mostly from the MEK inhibitor given in combination with a BRAF inhibitor. Rare, but serious hemorrhage events including GI bleeding, intracranial hemorrhage and hematuria are described in association with
L2260: use of these agents. The FDA therefore conducted analyses to assess bleeding/hemorrhage events across the pediatric and adult single agent safety pools.
L2261: Across the pediatric pool, 53% (39/74) of patients had some bleeding event including the following preferred terms: anal hemorrhage, ecchymosis, epistaxis, hematochezia, hematoma, hematuria, lower gastrointestinal hemorrhage, menorrhagia, oligomenorrhea, and rectal hemorrhage. Among these, 22% (16/74) had a bleeding event that was considered at least possibly related to selumetinib exposure including epistaxis (n=9) hematuria (n=5), anal/rectal hemorrhage (n=1) hematochezia and lower GI hemorrhage (n=1). All events were nonserious and Grade 1-2 in severity. There were two Grade 3 bleeding AEs that were considered unrelated to selumetinib: hematoma and hematuria, each in one patient, each resulting in interruption but no dose reduction.
L2262: Across the supportive adult hyd-sulfate pool, 14% (11/79) of patients experienced a bleeding event including the following preferred terms: anal hemorrhage, ecchymosis, epistaxis, GI hemorrhage, hematemesis, hematuria, hemorrhagic anemia, and vaginal hemorrhage. All events were Grade 1-2 except for two Grade 3 events of GI hemorrhage and serious hematemesis. Only one bleeding event (epistaxis) was considered related to selumetinib. Across the larger adult monotherapy pool of 347 patients, there were 3 fatal bleeding events: hemoptysis in a patient with NSCLC, ruptured aortic aneurysm which occurred 35 days after the last dose of selumetinib, and cerebral hemorrhage in a patient with brain metastases. The investigators attributed these events to underlying disease or pre-existing conditions and unrelated to selumetinib.
L2263: Among pediatric patients in the externally-sponsored research studies, there were four patients with serious bleeding events including Grade 3 hematuria (1), Grade 2 intracranial hemorrhage (2) and Grade 2 hemorrhoid hemorrhage (1). These patients all had underlying low grade glioma. The investigator assessed the Grade 2 hemorrhoid bleeding as related to selumetinib. The other events were considered unrelated. Among pediatric patients in the PN expanded access program, there were 3 serious hematuria events, no grading available, none resulting in dose modifications. One event was considered related to selumetinib.
L2264: Based on review of the available data, common bleeding events that occurred in SPRINT will be included in Section 6 of the label.
L2265: ## 8.2.5.9 Weight Gain
L2266: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2267: Selected AEs of weight increased were considered to be other significant adverse events (OAEs) based on the fact that these events were managed through selumetinib dose modification by the investigator.
L2268: A total of 3 patients had an OAE of weight increased, 2 of which were considered causally related to selumetinib by the investigator. All of the events were CTCAE Grade 3, considered to be non-serious, and resulted in selumetinib dose modification: 2 patients had an interruption and 1 patient had an interruption followed by a dose reduction. Two patients recovered, and the outcome was unknown for the remaining patient.
L2269: Based on the review of data from adult studies (Study 8 and Study 12), weight gain in these patients was attributed to confounding factor of edema. Bekaii-Saab et al (2011) have shown that in a Phase II Metastatic Biliary Cancer study, patients treated with selumetinib gained an average nonfluid weight of 8.6 pounds. The authors attributed this observation to the possible immune-modulation properties of selumetinib. However, the incidence of weight increased that has been observed in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 (5 of 50 patients; 10%) is consistent with the frequency observed in a cross-sectional study reviewing the weight of children and adolescents (age range 0 to 19 years) with NF1 (6 of 81 patients; 7.4%) (Rockwell 2001).
L2270: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L2271: The FDA analyzed the pediatric pool database for weight gain that was considered a treatment emergent AE. Across the 74 patients included in the pediatric NF1 PN pool, 14% (10/74) experienced an AE of increased weight including six patients (8%) with Grade 3 weight increase AEs. One weight increase AE resulted in permanent discontinuation and two patients had dose reductions. The FDA reviewed the summary narratives of the three patients in SPRINT Phase II Stratum 1 who required dose modifications due to weight increase AEs and there were confounding variables and unknowns (e.g., diet changes) in all cases. The literature reference provided to support the assertion that weight gain in SPRINT was consistent with that of the NF1 general pediatric population is based on a cross sectional study in Brazilian children with NF1 to primarily evaluate head size, stature, birth weight and weight. The study had a number of limitations including small sample sizes and retrospective analyses. The FDA contends that reliable conclusions regarding selumetinib's effect on weight in the pediatric study population cannot be made based on the available data. This potential safety signal could be addressed with longer term follow-up of patients in the post-marketing phase.
L2272: The FDA additionally notes that the Rockwell 2001 reference listed in the Applicant's position statement is an error. The reference should be (Ribeiro and Coutinho 2015).

L2275: Refer to Section 8.1.2.
L2276: ## 8.2.7. Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
L2277: ## **Data:**
L2278: See the Applicant's Position below.
L2279: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L2280: ## **Intrinsic Factors**
L2281: There were no clinically meaningful differences in the safety profile of selumetinib with respect to age, gender and race in the Pediatric Pool. Though some differences were observed, it is difficult to draw conclusions based on the small number of pediatric patients in the analysis (N=74) and the disproportionate impact of the difference of a few patients between subgroups.
L2282: No dose adjustment for any patient subgroup is considered necessary, and therefore not recommended in the proposed labelling. Evaluation of these safety data did not indicate the need for any additional safety monitoring over that already outlined in the proposed labelling as part of routine risk minimization.
L2283: ## **Effect of Age**
L2284: An evaluation of AEs, AEs of CTCAE Grade 3 or higher, SAEs, AEs of special interest, suggested that there were some differences between pre-pubertal patients (\ge 2 to 11 years of age) and post-pubertal patients (12 to 16 years of age) in the Pediatric pool. Dry skin and pyrexia were more prevalent in children \ge 2 to 11 years; dermatitis acneiform occurred at a higher frequency in the 12 to 16 years age group. However, these findings were not considered clinically significant, nor do they represent a pattern and may be likely caused by variability within a small single-arm study.
L2285: In the Adult monotherapy pool, although the incidence of SAEs was higher in the older population (\ge 65 years of age), there were no differences in incidence of &gt;10% for any individual AE.
L2286: In population PK analysis, based on BSA derived dosing, there was no clinically relevant difference in exposure of selumetinib due to patients' age.
L2287: ## **Effect of Gender**
L2288: An evaluation of AEs, AEs of CTCAE Grade 3 or higher, SAEs, AEs of special interest, suggested that there were some differences between male and female patients in the Pediatric pool; AEs of anaemia, hypertension and sinus tachycardia were reported at a 2-fold higher frequency in male patients whereas AEs of alopecia (reported term: hair thinning) and hair colour changes were reported at a 2-fold higher frequency in female patients.
L2289: Based on population PK analysis, gender has no clinically relevant impact on selumetinib exposure.
L2290: ## Effect of Race
L2291: In both the Pediatric pool and the Adult monotherapy pool, the number of patients in the non-White group was too small to allow a reliable comparison and evaluation of any meaningful differences with the White group.
L2292: Increased systemic exposure (approximately 1.5 to 2-fold) has been observed in adult healthy volunteers of Asian subjects, although there is considerable overlap in the range of exposure experienced for both Asian and Western subjects when corrected for body weight. It is anticipated that the observed PK ethnic differences of selumetinib are likely not to be of clinical relevance, particularly as pediatric patients will follow a selumetinib dosing regimen based on BSA (see Section 6.3.1). (b) (4)
L2293: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2294: The FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment of safety subgroup analyses for age, gender, and race in the pediatric pool and emphasizes the limited sample sizes for these analyses. Please refer to Section 6.2.2.2 for a discussion of age and gender effects on PK exposures.
